ParlaMint-IT_2016-01-20-LEG17-Senato-sed-563.ana.u9	The next item is the continuation of the debate on the draft Constitutional Law No 1429-D, already approved in first resolution by the Senate, first amended by the Chamber of Deputies, again amended in first resolution by the Senate and approved without modifications in first resolution by the Chamber of Deputies.
ParlaMint-IT_2016-01-20-LEG17-Senato-sed-563.ana.u10	Madam President, I would like to thank my colleagues for their attention. I, like all my colleagues, am here to try to bear witness to this reform of the Constitution. It is an important testimony that we tried yesterday to give in a disconcerting loneliness in this House. However, this is of great importance, especially in view of a referendum. Despite the importance that this debate should begin to have, yesterday in Commission 1a everyone was not allowed to make their statements. 15 senators couldn't do their surgery. Last time it was said that there were too many amendments and they could not be discussed. The amendments were withdrawn, but there were still too many. Even one would have been too much, since no opposition amendment and certainly no 5 Star Movement amendment has passed. I will talk about the forms, procedures and method, which always imply the substance of the facts. It is important to speak, because here we have come to empty things and leave only the formality of making oppositions speak in an empty Chamber where the other Members are not present and do not even bother to listen to the reasons. There is no comparison; there is no listening. What exactly is happening these days? That same deconstitutionalization that in 1994 was strongly desired by the right, but was then contained by a certain left. The same left that is now implementing the same project that in 2001, when it came to a small part of the Constitution, verified that the reform had not been very involved and stated that it would never do such a thing again. There was also a referendum that was not very well attended, and the left, then, could say that this was not a good thing. Yet, the opposite is the case today. There is therefore a transition from representative democracy, which we believe was ripe for becoming a direct democracy, to decision-making democracy. Why did this happen, since there was a time when someone in this country was fighting to go in another direction? Because it is other forces that dictate the agenda: these are international and supranational forces. Therefore, the Boschi of conflicts of interest and of the banks and Renzi, with its overlychism, are nothing more than instruments, gloves shod by others, more powerful. So what's going on? It is right to repeat it, because repeating it helps. The Constitutional Court, by ruling No. 1 of 2014, declared the unconstitutionality of the so-called Porcellum, under which the XVII legislature was elected. [[Brusio. References by the President]] , but the Government and the parliamentary majority did not respect the limits that this judgment imposed on the legislator. It happened that the Court, while declaring the Porcellum unconstitutional for some of its parts, i.e. for the majority prize, allowed the Chambers to continue to operate, but according to the principle of continuity of the State. In order to implement this extension, the Court expressly referred to two articles of the Constitution: Article 61, according to which the Chambers can continue their work until the new ones are elected, and Article 77, which provides for the possibility for the Chambers, even if dissolved, to be convened to convert the decree-laws into law, i.e. for urgent regulation. In fact, I remind all of you that the decrees-laws are provided for precisely in cases of urgency. Well, this principle of continuity of the State meets very short time limits: no more than three months. It was said, however, that it was not possible and that the international and national economic contingency did not allow elections to take place in three months. Perhaps it could have been done in a year; but no! One year would have been enough to revise the electoral law and return to the polls, to finally have a legitimate and constitutional parliament. On the other hand, it has been thought of as an institutional gamble, i.e. the revision of the Constitution has taken place, with men and women illegally sitting on the backs of this Parliament thanks to an unconstitutional majority prize. And what do these men and women do? They press buttons that make the difference between passing and not passing a certain measure, including constitutional reform. They are unelected but nominated men and women, blackmailable for their fear of losing a place that does not belong to them. When one has the perception of illegality, one is afraid of losing what - precisely - is not a right. In fact, thanks to this blackmail, the rules of the game have changed even before these were rules, i.e. a state of affairs has been implemented, it has already been given to establish, when it was not yet, at the strokes of demands of confidence votes and decree-laws. Today we are already living in a Parliament other than the one dictated by the Constitution, even if the Constitution does not prohibit certain practices, because it clearly leaves wide discretion; in fact, it is true that certain practices are not expressly prohibited, but the fact of systematically resorting to the vote of confidence and the decree-law imposing the will and the majority means that the game has already changed. Let's get back to the question of method. In addition to that analysis, there is the fact that the proposed revision of the Constitution at our examination is a governmental initiative. It is a clear anomaly, even this one which is not explicitly prohibited, but which does not fall within the logic of things. What does the Government have to do with the executive power that should deal with the actual implementation of the laws enacted by Parliament, with the revision of the Constitution? It's got nothing to do with it! He was supposed to be a spectator. In fact, it is not by chance that the Constitutional Court, that is, the supreme body that supervises the respect of the Constitution, is appointed by Parliament, the President of the Republic and the judiciary, but not by the Government. Yet this is happening. Then there is another bad anomaly represented by the fact of comparing, of comparing in parallel the permanence in Renzi's political life with the victory in the referendum. So the life of a political man, not elected and absolutely very questionable, is being put on the same footing, with what should be the Constitutional Charter of all Italians. This is not just a draft law, it is a momentary decision, but - I repeat - the Constitutional Charter. He is asking explicitly that the referendum be related to Renzi's permanence in politics: to express a yes or a no for the Constitution will be to give a yes or a no for Renzi. This is unacceptable, megalomaniac and childish! It's a real blackmail! What is the Constitution? Unfortunately, I am here to repeat it, because it seems that we have forgotten it: it is a tool to limit power and avoid abuse and to avoid first and foremost the concentration of power in the hands of a few. Well, that is what we want to do: we want to destroy the architecture of the Constitution precisely because it bears these values and we denounced it as early as July 2013, after a report by JP Morgan, a private financial company, which made an analysis of the European Constitutions and evaluated them too liberal, too socialist, because they predicted weak executives against parliaments, because they protected workers' rights too much and allowed the license to protest. Then we asked a question and asked if the Government was aware of this report of a private financial company from overseas and asked the Government to give a clear answer in defence of our Constitution. We did not get an answer to this question, the JP Morgan did with this government bill. Thank you. I understand, too. What, then, is the architecture of the Constitution? It provides for a clear horizontal and vertical division. The horizontal division is between legislative, executive and judicial power. For those who do not remember it, a net division of the three powers is absolutely necessary to maintain and guarantee democracy and after the adoption of this bill will no longer be so clear. The vertical division is between the authorities: the State, the Regions and the Local Authorities. Then it provides for the presence of guarantee bodies, precisely the Constitutional Court and the President of the Republic, but also in this field it gets a hand. In short, the whole process that has characterized the debate and the preparation of this bill denotes a lack of constitutional culture and where it is not indiscipline is dole, because I don't think anyone realizes the seriousness of this proposal and whoever realizes it is guilty. A majority vote was taken. Again, I would like to remind all of you that in 2001 the constitutional reform of the Centre-Left was defined by all as an experience not to be repeated and some of you were certainly there to say these things. The 1948 Constitution was approved by more than 90 percent of the consensus. The Renzi Government, on the other hand, in this parliamentary process does not have the consent of anyone; it does not have the consent of the oppositions and even some parliamentarians of the majority manifest some doubts, or at least manifested them. Then what happened? These regurgitations of conscience, of ethics, have been silenced. There is always a coin, an oboleum, that serves to silence consciences, to make us feel at peace and return home satisfied. It happened with the eligibility and ineligibility of senators. In Article 57, one paragraph contradicting the other tells us that perhaps yes, the senators will be elected even if it is not known how they will be fished by a cauldron of elected local authorities. However, many of you have felt that this has been a fair remedy for your perplexity and you have moved forward. But the rules of the democratic game must be changed together, because the Constitution is not an instrument that is the subject of political battles. The current majority is, therefore, altered by this majority prize. This distortion, which is already allowing the manipulation of the Constitution, together with the electoral law with which it mates - the Italicum - will expect an even worse situation, because it will allow, black and white, similar majority prizes and will certainly allow a party that can take even a quarter of the votes of the voters (and not of those entitled, therefore really a miserable part of the total) to command over all. There is, then, this referendum that does not enter into the specific of the bill, because it does not deal with the functions of the Senate, the method of election of the senators, the election of the President of the Republic, the senators of presidential nomination, the competences of the Regions, which now remain without competence: the voters will be asked to express only a yes or a no, there is no way of diversifying. Madam President, allow me to speak for another minute, because I would also like to point out a few points in relation to the procedure that this constitutional revision has had. First of all, I recall the removal of three senators in June 2014 from the Senate's Constitutional Affairs Committee: Senator Mauro, Senator Mineo and Senator Chiti; since they had invoked respect for freedom of conscience, they were relieved of office. There is a second question that leaves me very perplexed. PRESIDENT. - The debate is closed. I already gave her an extra minute. Make sure you finish. NUGNES (M5S). I'm gonna finish. At first reading there were Senator Finocchiaro as a majority rapporteur and, as a minority rapporteur, Senator Calderoli; at second reading only Senator Finocchiaro remained and no longer Senator Calderoli: the minority rapporteur failed. Last but not least, in October 2015, the vote on the Cociancich amendment, which was structured in such a way as to preclude a whole series of votes which would have required a secret vote. I must say that we experienced a great, high moment in this House when that amendment came out. [[Applause from the M5S Group]]
ParlaMint-IT_2016-01-20-LEG17-Senato-sed-563.ana.u11	He's enlisted to speak Senator Scavone. You have the right to do so.
ParlaMint-IT_2016-01-20-LEG17-Senato-sed-563.ana.u12	Madam President, I would like to say that I am referring to the presentation of the speech in this House and I turn the text over to the documents.

ParlaMint-IT_2016-01-20-LEG17-Senato-sed-563.ana.u14	Senator Alicata is enlisted to speak. You have the right to do so.
ParlaMint-IT_2016-01-20-LEG17-Senato-sed-563.ana.u15	Madam President, ladies and gentlemen, the text that we are about to vote with a yes or a no in this last passage to the Senate is far from that presented by the Government. In that text you could find some of the guidelines of the constitutional reform of 2005 promoted by the Berlusconi government, such as the end of the perfect bicameralism and a Senate representative of regional realities. That reform of ours provided for the abolition of the concurrent competence between the State and the Regions, which, approved by the constitutional changes of the left of 2001, has created, in more than a decade, a vast dispute between the State and the Regions at the Constitutional Court. That dispute weighed heavily, delaying the development of the country. All the changes made during this parliamentary process, which was long but lacking in real confrontation, did not resolve the critical aspects of the reform; on the contrary, a text was trivialized which, on the other hand, had to be deepened, not achieving the objectives that were intended to be pursued. The path of dialogue undertaken with the main opposition party, which was not a real articulated one but an attempt to share reforms, was not only trampled on, but even trivialized. The result is just a trivial and useless reform because written by the majority, overcoming a serious confrontation with the opposition. With regard to the functions assigned to the new Senate, those assigned to him attempt to achieve the overcoming of the perfect bicameralism, modifying the institutional weight of the Second Chamber. The aim of the reform is to exclude the Senate from the trust circuit - trust is only requested from the House - and to reduce its weight in the legislative process. But while the Senate is being transformed into a representative assembly of territorial authorities, a highly centralistic reorganization of the form of State is being carried out and therefore a clear marginalization of its role. The compensation that has been tried to implement towards the role of the Senate, increasing the number of bicameral laws, continues to highlight a form of government that remains parliamentary, although having excluded, as said, the Senate from the trust circuit. Much of the Senate's functions, such as that on the evaluation of public policies and activities of public administrations, are diminished precisely because they attributed to a second Chamber to which the trust bond with the Government has been severed, just as it is ambiguous - and it will require regulatory action for its implementation - the provision for the election of 95 senators. It is not clear how the choices made by the voters for the candidate advisers can be made: a very confused and indeterminate expression that will create an institutional confusion between the roles of mayors, councillors, Presidents of the Region and senators. We are also faced with a mess where the municipalities are expected to be represented in the Senate, but their representatives will not be chosen by the citizens or even by the municipalities themselves, but by the regional councils; with what criterion is not given to know. Much is left to the implementing law, but it would have been more appropriate to define matters immediately. Even on the timing of implementation of the bicameral law governing the election of senators remains a fundamental contradiction between the two terms provided for in the same article of the bill. The question arises as to whether six months from the date of the election of the Chamber or six months from the date of entry into force of the constitutional law. A great confusion, in short, unless the real intention of the majority was simply to dequalify the second House, which would no longer find its legitimacy in a direct election, among other things clearly violating Article 1 of the Constitution, which provides that sovereignty belongs to the people. The further element of inequality is that of the appointment of five senators by the President of the Republic, no longer for life, but for a period of seven years. To these are added those by right, the former Presidents of the Republic, who continue to be members of the Senate for life. The aim is also that, declared in the title of the bill, to reduce costs, but in reality the costs, evaluated from several parts, do not diminish at all and this remains only a trivial, trivial argument of tried propaganda. A close connection must then be recorded between this constitutional reform, which strongly alters the system of weights and counterweights provided for by the current Constitution, and the new electoral law, the so-called Italicum, modelled to be applied to the Chamber of Deputies alone. Precisely the overcoming of equal bicameralism and the redefinition of a series of powers at the head of the Government and of the functions of the Chamber, which is the holder of the trust relationship with the Executive, and which is elected by giving the winning list a strong majority prize, substantially alter the system of weights and counterweights in favor of the Executive, putting at serious risk precisely that constitutional engineering created by the constituent Fathers to prevent it from being a power to subject others. The allocation of powers so wide to an executive supported by the winning list, which benefits from the majority prize, but which could be a minority in the country, gives us very disturbing aspects not only for the quality of the laws that would be produced for citizens, but also for our democracy. It is precisely the reforms we are examining that are a clear example of this, if a minority (the Democratic Party) who has obtained the majority prize declared unconstitutional can change our Constitution in a very serious way. The reform instead had to be written by a large parliamentary group just to be more serious, more authoritative and lasting and this precisely because the decision of the Constitutional Court on the so-called Porcellum has delegitimized the majority prize, and therefore the action of the Democratic Party, reducing its political and moral legitimacy. Thus, the limits to the possibility of constitutional revision dictated by the Constitution itself and by constitutional jurisprudence in multiple judgments have been overcome by majority shots, but even the political and moral limits that legitimize such a wide-ranging rewriting of the Constitution. [[Applause from Senators Comrade and Malan.]]

ParlaMint-IT_2016-01-20-LEG17-Senato-sed-563.ana.u17	Madam President, this is not an easy speech for me, so I would ask you to be patient. As far as the substance of this reform is concerned, I would ask you to refer back to my speech in this House on 15 July 2014 because I do not change anything of the things I said then with regard to my opposition. However, I followed the general debate until the end of the House's work this morning and I would like to say a few words to remind the friends and friends of the opposition that, in democracy, you can also experience the misfortune of being in the majority one day. And then all the verbal excesses, all the attacks away from the common sense and logic of the parliamentary debate will come back as boomerangs. So, I think it would be good for everyone to moderate their tones. Today's speeches about dictatorships, people tied to armchairs, and other things like that might come back someday. As I said once - but I want to repeat this because I am convinced that it is the real problem of our country - anti-politics is a problem that is being tackled in all the countries of Europe. From us there is a difference, and it is a dramatic difference that should make everyone reflect and consists in the fact that anti-politics has achieved such violence as to become an anti-institution: our people today have a rejection of the institutions. Therefore, the attacks on Senator Micheloni (I speak of myself to avoid references to others, but the principle applies to all parliamentarians and politicians) are justified, they are part of the rules of democracy. But when these attacks, this anti-politics falls on the institutions, then no one controls anything in a country and then the roads that open are unknown and dangerous. I would therefore like to express a wish that everyone should lower their voices when it comes to this subject. Then there is something that worries me a lot. For several months we have all been bombarded in the e-mails about civil unions. For Christ's sake, this is a serious problem, which concerns some tens of millions of our citizens. Personally, I have not suffered such attacks on constitutional reform. I am very concerned about this aspect of the Members' everyday life, because it shows how sensitive our people are to us and to our institutions. This does not remove anything from my opposition to this reform of the Constitution. I announce here that I will campaign against this law, I will join the referral committees for no and I can also announce that abroad, where I live, in Europe, committees for no are being prepared. But why am I against it? As I said, I spoke about the subject in 2014 and the things said remain valid. I am opposed because I am convinced that the system proposed to us today does not work for the country, is dangerous for our country and will not guarantee the cohesion of the nation. This is the other problem we have in Italy: we do not have the sense of the State, we do not have the sense of belonging to a national community. Well, this system aggravates the problem and this reform, in my opinion, does not respond to the distance that has been created between the citizen and the institutions, on the contrary, I believe it increases this aspect. That is the reason for my opposition. Is reform necessary? Yes, a reform is necessary. Many colleagues have made much better efforts than me in proposing interventions to reform our Constitution, modernise it and make the country more governable by overcoming bicameralism. We have put forward all the proposals and they have not been accepted. However, when it is said that something is not working, a French proverb comes to mind, which reads: "The bad workers always have bad tools" (I don't know if that's what they say in Italian). Perhaps then, if the system does not work, it is because we are not good workers in this system and we should think a little more about ourselves. Finally, I want to touch on an aspect that highlights the limitations and problems of the system if it is put up in this way. I have been told an information that I do not want to believe; I consider it a monumental hoax, however, hoping that it is a hoax, the fact that someone thought of it should make us worry about how this system can one day be used. If you remember, as an elected member of the foreign college I had proposed to remove the MPs from the House that gives confidence to the Government, because I would consider a step forward for the community that I represent here to be present only in the Chamber that does not give confidence to the Government but that expresses needs, directives and proposals for action, released from the relationship of trust with the Government. This proposal has not been accepted either. Well, the bufala that circulates is the following (and, I repeat, I really hope that I have been intoxicated by this information): it seems that an agreement is under discussion between the MAIE - Italian associative movement abroad, which has here a senator and two or three Members of the House - and Mr Verdini to obtain a modification of the electoral law that gives us the obligation to reside abroad, providing applications abroad, in Europe, through the MAIE, for some people. I repeat: I hope it is a hoax, however we will understand it if we are proposed to amend that law. This is worrying for the underlying thought. It would not have occurred to me, so I am admired by the inventive abilities of those who think these things, but this is one of the risks. I will conclude by making it clear that I will vote today and I do not want to hide why I am not announcing it now: out of respect for my history, out of respect - and I address my colleague, Mr Nugnes, who says that we have only had regurgitation or that we are tied to these seats - of my wife and children, who lived without me because I lived for politics. I have lived for politics and not for politics and my family has lived for my work as a builder in Switzerland and not for what I perceived in politics and today, as several colleagues (I see one in front of me), we are here to lose money because we can afford it, having worked before. Out of respect for these people, I cannot take the risk - at least at this time and I want to understand exactly - of being the one tonight to ensure that this reform passes with some vote of another political force and not of the party to which I still belong. That is the doubt I still have. There is no doubt about the reform and what I have announced about my intention to campaign against it, but this has nothing to do with the relationship of trust with the Government. I would also like to make it clear to the colleagues with whom I fought in the first readings that if I have to reproach this Government for something, I am reproaching the inadequacy of some decisions and actions that have been taken. The jobs act to me has disturbed because it has not yet brought us into a North European system of job management. So it's not okay for me, not for the same reasons I always read in the press. It's not good for me because it hasn't modernised the rules of work as it is in the whole of Northern Europe, an area of our interest because it's in competition with us and with which we have to compete as a country. This government, the government I support, doesn't suit me because in the public administration it didn't do enough. We have long been proposing the reform of the Ministry of Foreign Affairs and there is no shadow of it. That's why I have problems with the current government, not because of opposition or lack of trust. Therefore, tonight I will take on my responsibilities, as I have always done in my life. Then, within my party, who has to decide what future relations will be. PRESIDENT. - The debate is closed. Senator Petrocelli is enlisted to speak. You have the right to do so.
ParlaMint-IT_2016-01-20-LEG17-Senato-sed-563.ana.u18	Good morning Madam President and good morning to my colleagues, senators and senators, and especially good morning to the Italians who listen to us on this day, as they heard yesterday, and who usually follow the Senate's work. But is it really going to be a good morning? I doubt it, really. Like Mr Micheloni, who just preceded me, I will not repeat the reasons why I doubt that this will be remembered as a good day. In fact, in the last act of parliamentary passages relating to constitutional counter-reforms, I just want to stress, at the beginning of the speech that I will make with these last 20 minutes of reflection, that despite everything I am still convinced that it is counter-reform. It is a good thing that historians and history will establish the adjective best suited to the path we are unfortunately pursuing, because it is history that gives the definitive titles to the legal provisions. In fact, as far as the President of the Council can call Unlock Italy a measure, it will be the history of our country and of the European Union and it will be the material conditions of those who live and live there to tell us if this path can really represent an unblocking for our country and have positive effects on the living conditions of its citizens and on the whole economy or if instead it will produce bad conditions, as I am afraid. It is a good thing that it is not enough, with an activity of pure propaganda, to put roboating titles to legislative interventions so that history remembers to rename them as they deserve best. Our country has examples. A counter-reform like the one we are releasing resembles many other propaganda reforms that we have seen over the centuries, starting from the Council of Trent and what happened in those years, then judged for what they were giving them the right label. And not just in this area. We are an example of terms that certainly did not have negative connotations when they were coined. Here I want to remember, and the reference is not purely random, the term fascism, that at the moment of its conception had no negative connotation, but that today we know well what heavy consequences of tears and blood has produced over the years and that still today we drag ourselves behind as an issue never completely resolved in our country. I repeat, I do not quote a random term, my quote is deliberately not random. What we have before our eyes is a path already analyzed quite thoroughly. I will not be here to repeat things already said in the previous passages, but I want to make two distinctions: what could happen if we have only one man in command in the coming months and years and, briefly, what many, in this last period and in any case in this legislature, have said about this path of constitutional counter-reforms. I will recover some passages, some reflections made by others, some positions of mine and of my Group. I start from a general consideration that I would summarize in the following sentence: are these counter-reforms a pretext or a reality? And from this I would start to say that objectively I believe that the path we are facing, whether it is a pretext or a reality, it seems to me that it serves to keep up the current Government, the previous and the one that has proceeded even before, in a context in which it governs a main contradiction, that between money and work. We are not going to delete a written Constitution in such a way that there was an explicit reference to the work proper to the first article of the 1948 Constitution. I am talking about the contradiction between money and work, because I do not feel like recovering old clichés such as those of the contradiction between capital and labour: here we are precisely the contradiction between monetarism and work. I believe that this contradiction is really becoming apparent, and I do not believe that there is a better excuse, from the time of the appointment of the fifteen essays, to make the path of so-called constitutional reforms, which I continue to call counter-reform, the prince pretext to buy time and to keep up all the real and supposed majorities that followed in the seventeenth legislature. I remember that more than anyone has written, in these three years or so of legislature, that this pretext of constitutional counter-reform probably served to keep up their government majorities, at least - I would add - until one day the real intentions of those who are really managing the reform or counter-reform process, that is, the needs of the Government of Chancellor Merkel. When those demands, which are also the needs of the most reactionary part of the European Union, are clear, it will not be at all important that these so-called constitutional reforms, and I am also saying electoral reform, have been done for real or for fake purposes. And here I come back to the main point: pretext or reality? At that point I am convinced that it will survive, in the institutions of our country, but also between the social partners, the unions, the social relations between the people who live in this country, who will have been able to engage in the rules imposed by the European Union and its supranational institutions. For the rest, probably for more than half of Italian society, there will be only one word of comfort, which perhaps will come in a way unfortunately sometime from Pope Francis. I now want to remind you that many people have said in this legislature that the capacity of our 1948 Constitution was basically to hold together different political cultures (I will not dwell on the methods and means used then to hold these different cultures together). This recognition that was given to the 1948 Constitution has now been completely erased. At that time the country had a sort of biodiversity, not only social, but above all political. This biodiversity, in the counter-reform that this Parliament is approving, is being transformed. I would rather talk about a Constitution that uses the concept of competitiveness, replacing it with that of political and social biodiversity. This too comes from supranational institutions, especially those not voted by citizens. I am not talking about the European Parliament, which, in my view, is not doing its job as it should. Replacing the concept of competitiveness (a word that has roots in economic and financial mechanisms) to that of political biodiversity (which identified the 1948 Constitution) is the biggest damage that the operations carried out by this Government could do to our Constitutional Charter. This passage, consisting in replacing competitiveness with others, has its roots in 2003, in the path that started precisely in those years. This is a passage which I do not consider irrelevant. Basically, what does it do? It means that the Constitution that we will approve - for those who will approve it, I will, of course, vote against it - loses much of its capacity for social aggregation, compensating it precisely with an electoral competitiveness, which, in my modest view, is a phenomenon that presupposes the transition to economic dynamics rather than a purely political dynamic. It loses its political centrality in the country's fundamental Charter and replaces it with gruff operations; concepts, hypotheses, axioms that come precisely from the degeneration due to the fact that in this country and in Europe as a whole, the dominance of the culture of monetary and fiscal economy has taken over the culture of social coexistence and politics understood in its purest and simplest form, because purity, in this case, is synonymous with simplicity and not complication. This is what I believe has happened in this parliamentary term. But, if the man only at the command that will come - I hope he will never come, considering what could mean for the life of all Italian citizens - he will be called to govern the fate of this country following the dictates of inaccurate lobbying and economic-financial categories that dictate the rules from the top of a mandate that has not been granted to him by anyone, then I want to complete my speech with a perspective: what I see, what is the main failure that could lead man only to the command that will come to the things that are on the agenda of the political, economic and financial national and international chronicle of these days? I do so by referring to something which could happen in the coming months and which perhaps concerns me more than Mr Micheloni is concerned about the advent of anti-politics throughout Europe. I would not like to see the signals coming in these months on the possibility of Italian intervention in the North African scenario and, in particular, in Libya, turning into reality. The signals that come to me denounce a strong pressure of the European Socialist Party that is lobbying and that would like a part of the European countries and, in particular, our country, together with Greece, France and Spain, in action contrary to the now classic austerity policy, which, even today, is led by the German Government of Chancellor Merkel. And God does not want - for those who are believers - or the future desire - for those who, like me, are not believers - that black sacks begin to arrive, for the insane choices that will be dictated, imposed or suggested to our Government - and of which this process of counter-reform is the emblem - to ensure the credentials of reliability and stability, vis-à-vis the strong European powers. If the constituent Fathers of this reform will not be as proud, as were the constituent Fathers of the 1948 Constitution, I certainly believe that it will not be the fault of those who are opposing this counter-reform, but only of the laxity with which it was proposed to the country, which unfortunately will have very high prices to pay, in the near future. I believe that, in order to be consistent, after the adoption - which will be certain - of this Constitutional Charter, you should repeal Article 348 of the Criminal Code or the abuse of the profession. It is difficult to argue that a person cannot exercise a profession because he lacks a professional academic title when a Parliament is appointed by an unconstitutional and therefore untitled law (such as professionals) operates and changes the laws. If intervention on some legislative areas could be acceptable because it was done by state of necessity, as a matter of urgency, this is certainly not the case with constitutional reform: it is a reform that focuses power in a few hands, that does not shorten the distance between the government and the citizens, indeed expropriates them from the little of decision-making autonomy that was left to them. First of all, it should be borne in mind that the procedure laid down in Article 138 of the Charter of Fundamental Rights presupposes that the exercise of constitutional revision is for the members of the two Chambers representing the people, to whom sovereignty clearly belongs, in accordance with Article 1, who must be elected by a vote, clearly of the citizens, which is a personal vote, equal, free and secret, in accordance with Article 48 of the Constitution. Too bad that this is a Parliament in office in which Members have been proclaimed elected according to the application of mechanisms declared constitutionally illegitimate by the Constitutional Court in judgment No 1 of 2014, from which comes a clear and obvious lack of legitimacy of these two Chambers to proceed to the revision of the constitutional text and, even more so, to the modification of the entire II Part of it. The formal hearings have given rise to some very interesting points. In fact, of the seven constitutionalists who appeared, five clearly stated that the majority prize could not be validated. We are talking about 148 Members out of 630; 148 votes that today, in the House, have weighed on the vote on these constitutional reforms. These five constitutionalists also made clear the principle of continuity of the State, arguing that the Chambers could continue to operate thanks to this implicit principle but limited in time, as exemplified by the Court itself in that judgment with the reference to the extension provided for in Articles 61 and 77, second paragraph, of the Constitution which provides at most an extension of a few months but certainly not of an entire legislature. Professor De Fiores, for example, said that he doubted that Parliament was in the legal-constitutional condition to exercise the power of revision for two reasons, namely that an elected Parliament with an unconstitutional electoral law could not be entitled to reform the Constitutional Charter from which superior provisions derive, by contrast, its illegality, and that the constitutional revision is outside the principle of continuity of the functions of the constitutional bodies. In support of this thesis there is also Professor Sorrentino. These are not the only five constitutionalists who have expressed themselves in this sense, so much so that in those hearings the publication of a note, which had been published on Constitutional Comparisons, by Professor Gino Scaccia of LUISS, who argued that, at the time when the Constitutional Court declared the electoral law unconstitutional, beyond the legal issues, the political legitimacy of Parliament fails. In a further interview given last August by Professor Alessandro Pace, who is professor emeritus of constitutional law at the Faculty of Law of La Sapienza in Rome, he also spoke about the legitimacy of these Chambers with words that some restraint should Put it on. Despite what I have told you, and what has been said by the Constitutional Court and most scholars, you have moved forward, resulting in a strongly unbalanced reform in favour of the government. It is an even more obvious goal if you evaluate the combined arrangement of these reforms together with the Italicum electoral law you have conceived. Our parliamentary Republic, which has been left to us by far more wise than we constituent Fathers, will basically become a presidential Republic. In fact, the institutional axis in favour of the Commission will move. Just to be clear, we will have a government that with its President-in-Office of the Council will fully manage the agenda of parliamentary work, that is, decide which laws will be calendarized and which will be approved or not, and a House that, by majority, will execute. She will be a mere performer, also strong of that numerical majority that will be guaranteed by the majority prize that is inserted within the Italicum. It is a problem which, for those who are not in charge of the work, may not seem so nodal, but in reality it is. In fact, a reform of this kind cannot be done just as you did, so much so that it is packed with profiles of constitutional criticality. Well, with a small rule inserted precisely in the so-called Boccadutri law, sanatoria has been created and parties have been allowed to obtain such refunds, although the competent Commission checks provided for by law have not been carried out. ParlaMint-IT_2016-01-20-LEG17-Senato-sed-563.ana.u24	Senator Cioffi is enlisted to speak. You have the right to do so.
ParlaMint-IT_2016-01-20-LEG17-Senato-sed-563.ana.u25	Madam President, we could begin this little reasoning with the words of Mr Boschi when he first came to the Commission, the first day he came to explain to us what constitutional reform was in his head. It was very interesting, because for 35 minutes he read a document that probably someone had written to her (he did it without showing a particular soul, as we read the task) and during this reading, at a certain point, he said that since the Senate was perceived by public opinion as a duplication perhaps it was necessary to go beyond and to overcome the bicameralism equal. It was very interesting to hear these words from Minister Boschi, because he immediately revealed what the mantra was, their motive, their thought: 'is perceived by public opinion'. Do we make a constitutional "cracker" because the Senate is perceived by public opinion as a useless duplication? That is why we are intervening in a fundamental part of the coexistence of all citizens, because that is what people want. Perhaps we should have explained to all uninformed citizens, but that we know that we are not fools, as the government and the majority who support it believe, that what they are trying to do is nothing but the usual centralization of power in the hands of the few. We should ask ourselves why we want to concentrate power. The power is centered, always according to the mantra used, because so we are more efficient, we make the laws faster, we make decisions that do not take at the moment because this country is blocked precisely by the fact of not making decisions. But are we sure this is the good of the citizens? Perhaps in Italy - we have said this several times in this House, always talking about constitutional reform - we have the problem of having too many laws, we probably do them wrong and they are also misspelled. Even a normal citizen who has to read the Constitution would be in trouble. We know that the Constitution should be written quite simply so that it is understandable to everyone. Those who read the text of a Constitution must be able to understand it immediately, because it must be written as if it were addressed to a 10-year-old child. It would be nice for the Constitution to be so simple and clear. Instead, in this text, people who have probably studied - perhaps they have graduated and someone may have even done a master's degree - do not really understand what is written and sometimes I get doubts too, because it is a continuous mixing, confusing. As I said before, however, one aspect is clear: centralization of power. There is no need to reiterate what it means to have fakely abolished the Senate and to have centralized the legislative function in the hands of a single Chamber, which will be elected, as we all know, with the Italicum, in which the famous majority prize will put in the hands of the majority party, in this absolute case, the power to make laws, to appoint the judges of the Constitutional Court, to elect the President of the Republic and all the guarantee bodies. When power is centered so strongly, as has already been said, we move towards what we might call a regime. But is a monarchist system, of regime or dictatorial, pretendingly because there is a semblance of democracy, more desirable than a system such as the one in which weights and counterweights are present in order to achieve the objectives we have set ourselves? Perhaps we should talk about what we want to achieve, what situations happen and which could lead us in this direction. Of course, when I say "we" I mean everyone except us in the 5 Star Movement. It is almost useless to specify this, so perhaps I should use the "voi" instead of the "noi", also because the "voi" fits us in a system of regime. What happens in the world? Many things happen in the world and perhaps some of them need to be examined. For example, we all know and have read that Germany, together with Russia, has proposed doubling the Nordstream pipeline. For those who do not know, the Nordstream is that pipe that leaves from Russia, passes under the North Sea, arrives directly in Germany and supplies to the latter, and therefore to Europe, of which Germany is of course the strongest exponent, representative and important, 55 billion cubic meters. With the doubling, we're going to be 110 billion cubic metres. Russia also supplies Europe through another pipeline, called Yamal, which carries about 20 billion cubic metres. This means that Europe is basically fully provided, when the doubling of the Nordstream will be done, through Germany (which would be the new Ukraine). So we will have the transit of all the Russian gas, which provides, as we all know, about 30% of Europe's needs, through Germany. In addition, another interesting fact happens in Germany. In the Frankfurt Stock Exchange the first market has been opened where it is possible to deal with Chinese securities not in euros or dollars, but directly in yuan; it is the first market, outside mainland China, where this happens. It seems that Germany, which was divided into two (of course we all remember the division between the Eastern bloc and the Western bloc), is trying to approach the Eastern bloc. It also happens that in the USA, after forty years of blockade, the ban on exporting crude oil and the first cargo of crude oil on 20 January (now there), will arrive right in Germany passing through the port of Trieste, which is the largest port of oil transit of the Mediterranean. So the American oil, instead of following the routes of the North Sea, will pass in the Mediterranean, will arrive in Trieste and, through the pipeline, will bring the oil to Germany. On the other hand it happens that in the United States the Volkswagen scandal is first discovered, undoubtedly an important industry in Germany and then there is rumor of a class action against Deutsche Bank: the old game of stick and carrot is evident to everyone. Another interesting thing that is happening, as we all know, is that the United States is trying to contain China: the famous " pivot to Asia," which is the American defence strategy in the China Sea. Of course, China responds with the string of pearls, which is the system that the Chinese have basically to counter with the pivot to Asia. As 80% of oil transport in China passes through the Malacca Strait, the Chinese have well thought of making an oil pipeline and a pipeline that passes through Burma and arrives directly in China. Now they are making the Pakistani economic corridor that will allow, through the port of Ghawar (which is 80 kilometers from Iran) to arrive, with an oil pipeline and a pipeline, directly to China in the North. In that port, which is Pakistani (remember that Pakistan is a nuclear power and historical ally of the USA), a naval base will be created, for now civil, Chinese; it is at the beginning of the Strait of Hormuz, which is the other essential transit point for oil trade. Another interesting point to verify is that Iran, after the end of sanctions, has made available to the world its immense gas reserves (37 trillion cubic meters) and these resources can go to East or West. If they go to the East they can follow the path of the Iran-Pakistan-India or Iran-Pakistan-China pipeline. The Indians, who had left the game to do a favor to the Americans, since the Chinese have said they will put the money to make the pipeline trade that is in Pakistan, have also said that they are willing to extend it to China; and after this happened the Indians have returned to the game. They said, one moment, we're interested in your gas too. On the other side we have two very interesting pipelines: the first is Qatar-Saudi Arabia-Jordan-Syria-Turkey and the other pipeline is Iran-Iraq-Syria: it happens, both pass through Syria, and happens to be a bit of a crisis in Syria. Clearly, if Iranian gas or Qatar gas arrives in Europe, the Russians lose their strength, because much of the gas sells it to Europe and therefore do not want it. This is perhaps the real reason for the conflict in Syria, because we want to stop the possibility of making these two pipelines. ParlaMint-IT_2016-01-20-LEG17-Senato-sed-563.ana.u28	Madam President, the constitutional revision has aged before it is born. It is turned to the past, seals the present, says nothing to the future of the country. The most important decisions are postponed or hidden or denied. The reduction in the number of regions is postponed. The cancellation of the Senate is hidden. The reduction in the number of Members is denied. The relationship between the State and the Regions becomes more difficult and more conflicting, since both levels have defined exclusive competences, which cannot therefore find any mediation after the cancellation of the concurrent legislation. Overcoming the small regions, on the other hand, would have created macro-regions more suitable to cooperate with national policy and move around the European area. The government has promised to implement them with a forthcoming constitutional revision, with a resounding admission that a non-binding law is being passed today. The new legislative procedure is cumbersome. Conflicts of competence are increasing and new disputes are being brought to light at the Consulta. Palazzo Madama becomes a post-worker for local administrators, a prestigious Assembly that will try to regain the lost powers by using the association exchange with the Government. If it had to fall so low, it was more dignified to abolish it. [[Applause from the M5S and Mixed Groups]] In a single Chamber it was inevitable to define the counterweights of the majority system: qualified votes on fundamental rights, powers of initiative of minorities, control of government activity. Well-tempered monocameralism is preferable to a messy bicameralism. The clumsy propaganda about the costs of politics has been bogged down in Transatlantic. There is still an anomaly of a House of 630 members, which has no equal in any European Parliament. An assembly as large as it is weak, whose members owe everything to the appointment of the head of the party or to the increase of the seats associated with the election of the Premier. On the other hand, the number of Members had to be reduced and selected from one-name colleges, without recourse to the Lords of Preferences and the nominations of Italicum; the House's authority towards the Executive should have been strengthened. American democracy, while leading an empire, has never renounced the balance of power between government and Parliament. Why, then, did he lose so many opportunities? The lack of genuine reform has produced a very long text, a poor legislative bill, an unpleasant bureaucratic jargon. Just read, for example, the additional competences of the Senate defined - I quote - in the laws referred to in Articles 57, sixth paragraph, 80, second period, 114, third paragraph 117, fifth and ninth paragraphs, 119, sixth paragraph, 120, second paragraph, 122, first paragraph, and 132, second paragraph. Yes, it is written just like this: it looks like a thousandprorogues decree, and instead it is a constitutional piece: a scribble that offends the simple and intense Italian of the Constituents. [[Applause from the M5S and Mixed Groups and Senator Volpi]] From the way you speak I understand what you want, it suggests popular common sense: the sloppy form reveals the low political profile. The vice of origin consists in changing the Constitution to stabilize a Government otherwise without the electoral mandate. The Porcellum had to be changed immediately to give back the floor to the voters. Instead, the contingent political need prevails over every institutional guarantee, until the paradox of rewriting the second part of the Charter in an elected Parliament in illegitimate form. In Italy one does not govern to improve the State; one changes the State to strengthen the Government. On the contrary, the Constitutions govern politics because they are long lasting. On this principle, the reforms of the last two decades, which have always been approved against each other, have failed to prop up the governments that had lost the confidence of the voters. So Berlusconi did in 2005 and - alas! - also my share with Title V. We repeat all the mistakes already made by the right and the left, putting us even more enthusiasm today, until we ask for a personal plebiscite. President Renzi is the great conservative of the second Republic. It erects a monument to the ideologies of the twenty years just as they set throughout Europe. It all began in the 1990s, with the hopes of some kind of Italian Westminster model. Today bipolarism is also in trouble in that ancient English palace; it no longer exists in Spain, it is overwhelmed in France by the lepenesimo, it is sterilized by the broad understandings in Germany. The Italicum and the Boschi law are eager to keep him artificially alive, using the absolute premiered without counterweights: a minority of 20% of those entitled to vote wins the bench and imposes their will on the majority of the country. All this increases abstentionism and reduces consent to bipolar competition. Everywhere the old dialectic between parties is overwhelmed by the new divide between the elite and the people. European countries have become ungovernable due to excessive governance. One simple truth has been forgotten: in order to lead today's fragmented societies we need a wider consensus than yesterday; political classes must learn to convince people rather than reduce representation; majority prizes in the long run cannot surrogate voters who do not vote. The policy has been reduced to mere system administration. This led to the leadership of the Executives over Parliaments. But on the European horizon the great dilemmas of peace and war, of the limits and links between religion and politics, of the reception and rejection of migrants, of individual freedom and public ethics, of the technological power and of the intangibility of life, of economic development and of the duration of the planet return. They are not problems solved by the Commission, they are contemporary conflicts that need new cultural and political recognition and will only be possible in a new parliamentary democracy as we have not yet known it. The one of the past century was able to neutralize the conflicts between the State and the Church, the city and the countryside, capital and labour. Parliaments are still to be imagined capable of reconstructing the fractures of European civilization in the new century. The world before us is very different from that of the nineties. The institutional reforms of the Second Republic are now old tools. The constitutional reform for the Italian future has not yet been written. ParlaMint-IT_2016-01-20-LEG17-Senato-sed-563.ana.u32	Senator Casini is enlisted to speak. You have the right to do so.
ParlaMint-IT_2016-01-20-LEG17-Senato-sed-563.ana.u33	Madam President, I asked to speak in the general debate on the draft Constitutional Reform Act, today before the Senate for the second parliamentary resolution, to testify to the reasons for my vote in favour. Since I joined Parliament in 1983, the public debate on constitutional reforms, which began in the 1970s, has focused on overcoming bicameralism, which has been a constant feature of the debate and has been the subject of many attempts to revise it. In this connection, I recall the establishment of the Bozzi Commission in 1983 and the successive attempts made by Parliament over the past thirty years: the De Mita-Iotti Commission in the 12th legislature and the D'Alema Commission in the 13th legislature, in which it was only possible to reform Title V of the Constitution. I also recall the attempt at constitutional revision approved by the Houses in the 14th parliamentary term, but not confirmed by the referendum, as well as the attempts at constitutional revision initiated and not concluded in the 15th and 16th parliamentary term. Over the last thirty years, therefore, the most advanced reformist bodies that have gone through the debate on constitutional policy have decidedly turned towards a model of bicameralism differentiated, in line with the parliamentary models of other European constitutional systems, in which the second Chambers perform different functions compared to the Political Chambers and follow criteria of differentiated compositions. It is, therefore, a choice that is certainly not impromptu, nor in any way based on the claim to achieve a democracy at zero cost, because the costs of democracy cannot be considered harmful: this is a dangerous demagogy. Let us be careful, ladies and gentlemen, because history is full of courses and appeals, and it is all too easy to remember the anti-parliamentary controversy that made an extraordinary contribution to the advent of fascism. But the choice is not impromptu and - I repeat - is not linked to the issues of the cost of politics, as far as I am concerned, but the result of a long and in-depth reflection, which has its roots in the same debate in the Constitutional Assembly. It reveals an awareness of the incompleteness and, in some respects, of the weakness of the choice made in favour of a perfect bicameral system, despite many authoritative members of that Assembly, among whom I would like to mention Constantine Mortati, expressed themselves in favour of a differentiated bicameralism. While the Political Chamber should have represented the indistinct representation, that is, the representation of the nation as a whole, the other Chamber should have offered a different channel of expression of representation, bringing to a central level diversified instances and interests, in particular those attributable to the territory. There is evidence of this debate in the current Constitution itself. Just read Article 57 of the Constitution which states, in the first paragraph, that the Senate is elected on a regional basis. It is certainly necessary to be aware that the text, now under examination by the Senate, is the result of a complex and articulated parliamentary process, which began in April 2014, and is not without its criticality. Firstly, the nature of the second Chamber remains uncertain, both as regards some of its functions and as regards its composition. With regard to the latter aspect, the composition of the body is based, as is well known, on an indirect electoral system: it will be the regional councillors who will elect the senators from among the members of the Regional Council itself and from among the mayors of the Region. At second reading, an important corrective was introduced to the Senate, which provides that indirect election should take place in accordance with the choices made by voters when renewing regional councils, thus anchoring the choices of the advisors to the popular will. There is a great deal of regret for me for not trying more courageously to inspire, even within a system of indirect election, well-known constitutional models. I refer, in particular, to the French model, in which senators are elected with an indirect electoral system, but where the active electorate is attributed to a broad audience of subjects, which gives the body a strong democratic legitimacy. As for the new functions assigned to the Senate, certainly qualified and open to possible developments that only experience will be able to confirm (in this sense it will be very important to establish the Senate and the connection between the Presidencies of the two Chambers), i.e. evaluation of public policies, verification and implementation of the laws of the State, control over the work of the public administrations, and their European projection, if well valued, they will be able to allow the Senate to be effectively, as well as a liaison body with the local self-governments, even - if not above all - a Chamber of interlocution with the Community institutions. A greater effort could be made in relation to the participation of the second Chamber in the legislative process. I know that President Finocchiaro has devoted a great deal of his energy to this end. The system defined by the new Article 70 of the Constitution foreshadows a system that could make the procedure for the approval of laws less fluid and favour possible conflicts between the two Chambers, since the procedures are diversified according to the object and type of the law, criteria that - as can be understood - can be the subject of different interpretations and therefore feed recourses to the Constitutional Court for vices in proceeding with the laws. As already emerged during several parliamentary passages, critical elements can also be found in other articles of the reform, not directly related to the issue of overcoming bicameralism equal. I am referring to the way in which the President of the Republic was elected, on which I spoke at second reading, and I am helping to ensure that the final decision - I have tried to do so - was as far as possible directed towards maintaining the independence and third-party features of which the Head of State - in the form of parliamentary government - cannot be deprived. Other criticisms, partly shared, have been made of the changes made to Title V of Part Two of the Constitution, concerning the establishment of the legislative powers of the State and the Regions. The possible centralist twist impressed on the order, especially with the inclusion of the so-called "premium clause," which allows the State to intervene, in the presence of certain conditions, on matters reserved for regional legislative competence, could further mortify the already strongly suffering regionalist authorities. However, it must be acknowledged that the intervention on that part of the Constitution has become necessary to correct some incompleteness and criticalities that the constitutional reform of 2001 has shown in recent years, so much so as to fuel a wide dispute before the Constitutional Court. In conclusion, ladies and gentlemen, any criticism does not seem to me to win over the reasons which lead me to give a favourable opinion on the text of the reform under consideration. We can no longer wait, as the President of the Republic, Giorgio Napolitano, has pointed out on several occasions. Too many were the failed attempts. A failure, again this time, could fuel an irreversible spiral of public distrust of the ability of political forces to reform the institutions. Moreover, it is to the citizens that we give the final responsibility for adopting this reform, in accordance with a clear and transparent procedure. Parliament has decided on a path, which the Italians will confirm or not. The Renzi Government has pushed this reform process and it is right that it has linked it and somehow links its destiny. But the Italians will have to choose not on the basis of misleading customizations, but on the merit of a reform that has been waiting for too long. [[Applause from the AP Group (NCD-UDC) and Senator Finocchiaro. Congratulations.]]

Thanks to the combination of constitutional reform and electoral law, as a result of the disproportionate majority prize, the party that wins the next elections, while not representing the majority of Italians, will have control of the Chamber of Deputies, will elect the President of the Republic on its own, will grant confidence to the Government and the President of the Council alone, will appoint the Presidents of all the Authorities and directly and indirectly control the majority of the members of the Constitutional Court and the Supreme Council of the judiciary alone. It is the end of that check and balance on which democracy is founded. Legislative power, executive power, judicial power in the hands of one party: it is the end of freedom. In the light of all this, I wonder once again where the constitutional flame that burned in the words of President Mattarella ended when, on 20 October 2005, as a Member of Parliament, he hurled himself against the reform of the Berlusconi Government, which, contrary to the proposal now under discussion, in addition to guaranteeing governance, also provided for indispensable mechanisms of institutional balance. Just now that it represents the highest authority to guarantee the Constitution, why, President Mattarella, is it silent in the face of such an attack on our freedoms? Well to her and to her colleagues of majority, effective and in some way enlarged, I would like to recall the words spoken by Don Sturzo in this Senate on 27 June 1957: "The Constitution is the foundation of the Republic. If it falls from the heart of the people, if it is not respected by the political authorities, if it is not defended by the Government and by Parliament, if it is tampered with by the parties, the hard ground on which our institutions are manufactured and anchored our freedoms will fail. I hope that these words, which are still echoing in this House so strong and current, will awaken the consciences of all those present to put the country's future ahead of the miserable political game. With today's vote the Senate could once again mortify not only itself, but the whole country, writing one of the poorest pages in the history of Italian institutions. I will never associate myself with such outrage; I will never write my name among those who have damaged the freedom of the Italians and I will emphasize it with my vote against, hoping that in the same way all those who can act in freedom of conscience will vote.
