ParlaMint-IT_2016-01-20-LEG17-Senato-sed-563.ana.u44	He's enlisted to speak Senator Chiti. You have the right to do so.
ParlaMint-IT_2016-01-20-LEG17-Senato-sed-563.ana.u45	Madam President, ladies and gentlemen of the Government, the text before us has not been modified in the House of Deputies. Therefore, unlike the first project, I confirm, as at the previous reading last autumn, my vote in favour. During the previous reading I explained the reasons for my consent. Overcoming the bicameralism of equality is a necessity for our democracy and I think that failure to do so would give a definitive blow to the credibility of politics in our country, in spreading across Europe the populisms that challenge representative democracy and the future of the European Union itself. The overcoming of equal bicameralism is now taking place, after the changes we made in the Senate last time, on grounds that are acceptable to me in substance. I refer first of all to the competences entrusted to the Senate, to the maintenance of equal bicameralism on the revision of the Constitution and referendums and to a major role of the Senate in the control of public policies and government appointments, tasks that can be of paramount importance in the relationship with the European Union and with the system of regions and local self-government. Of course, as always in democracy and in the reform of the institutions, the tasks must then be carried out, but to me it seems wrong and perhaps the result of an unupdated parliamentary culture that it is considered, for example, that functions of control over public policies or government appointments are something not relevant to a House. With regard to the subject of a more overall balance in the democratic system, I would like to point out that the procedures for the election of the President of the Republic, although it would have been desirable for me to have a more precise closing rule that we have not found, have removed this election from the force of a single party or a majority. From this point of view, I was amazed by Senator Quagliariello - I say this because I esteem him and have a cordial relationship with him - when yesterday he used the election of the President of the Republic as one of the reasons for his taking away, at least as a symbol of choice, in this passage of the vote to the Senate. Thus for the provision that the Senate elects the three judges of the Constitutional Court autonomously. These aspects, with the corrections that we have introduced, are, in my view, a step forward too. So, as far as the referendum is concerned, it refers to propositional and directional referendums. I am amazed that in the debate the speeches I have heard here or on television have not clearly highlighted the implementation of these instruments which have been introduced. This is correct, they must now be implemented, but the propositive and guiding referendums are a significant choice in the relationship between the institutions of democracy and citizens at a time when representative democracy needs to make its own and complementary moments and instruments of direct participation. Finally, for me, the source of legitimacy on which the Senate is based has been and remains decisive, namely the fact that regional councillors and senators will be chosen by the citizens. It is an innovation, also this, introduced in the previous passage to the Senate and confirmed in the House. Of course, I also believe that there are aspects for me, from my point of view, less convincing and I recalled them last time: non-bicameralism equal to the great ethical-political issues (I think and fear that we will understand it only in the coming years if this will be a risk of division and opposition in our country); the reduction, which I too had proposed with other colleagues, of the number of Members, the institutional location, that is, in which House, of the senators of presidential nomination, no longer for life but still elected for seven years, of the former Presidents of the Republic or, as my colleague Micheloni recalled, of the elected in the foreign college. However, I believe that the issues I have now referred to are certainly relevant, as are some aspects of the new Title V, but they are not prevalent, because of their weight, in order to make the reform process fail or to lead to the failure of the process: they exist, from my point of view - for others perhaps not - but I do not think that their weight can now be considered prevalent with respect to the landing place and the outcome of the reform process. Moreover, I too would have seen other solutions, as President Casini has just recalled: a rigorous Bundesrat model, that is, the Bundesrat as such (but it also involves a form of government and electoral law), or an overall and authentic French model, but - I would like to say to Senator Casini - this too not simply for the way the Senate is elected in France (the narrower body and competences), but also for the way it is elected, with a double-shift system and single-member colleges, the National Assembly. I want to say then that these elements, which are there, do not, however, lead me, as I said, to assume in conscience the responsibility of not supporting the approach of a path on which I think a significant part of credibility is played, not of one or the other political part, but of politics in our country. I do not want, in this speech, to go back to controversy with the parliamentary groups or with colleagues from other groups who, from their point of view, are opposed to the draft reform. In me there is a regret, which I consider to be a defeat for everyone: everyone can vary the burdens of responsibility, but I think it is a defeat for everyone to have failed to give life to a more widely shared reform. I would have liked a reform that would have marked, with the same course of the referendum (which however there will be and is a choice, it is not only a fact due to the numbers, and will give the citizens the last word on the reform), a new beginning in mutual recognition and between the parties and a reconstruction of trust between parties and citizens. I fear, hearing the tones and arguments of many speeches, that the referendum itself will lead not to a point of confrontation and a high moment of democracy for a comparison of merit on the different evaluations of the reform, but to the risk of a frontal political contrast that I do not think would be desirable. On the other hand, the path of delegitimization or of offense, even if done in a cordial way, does not highlight the strength of ideas, but rather their weakness and, above all, does not reconstruct a confidence in the relations between citizens and institutions. It says a proverb that sowing wind produces storm: storm, and not more advanced coexistence. Nor can it be argued that the Constitutional Court's ruling on the electoral law, still in force in 2013, would prevent Parliament from carrying out reforms (it should deal only with ordinary issues, then determining which they are). Is it ordinary to elect the judges of the Court? Is it ordinary to elect the Presidents of the Republic? It seems to me a path that does not make much sense and, above all, it is not the truth. If we do not tell each other what corresponds to the truth, it is difficult to rebuild something with the citizens. As I want to note, in conclusion, the attitude of colleagues from Forza Italia when expressing dissatisfactions, points of criticism, which lead them to raise their tones and the level of confrontation to testify their no to the reform. Even they talk about an attack on freedom and democracy. I think, however, that citizens will not follow this path, because the people who decide have as much awareness as we do about things. I repeat, I would remain on the subject, but above all, how do these colleagues justify voting for the reform when the innovation I referred to was not there? So today they vote because innovations are unsatisfactory or inconclusive, but they have already voted when they were not there. Just as they voted in favour of Italicum, when some of us have raised some reservations with regard to it, which were reservations not only, as Senator Quagliariello said yesterday, related to the possibility of introducing into the ballot the realization of a coalition, but also to the fullness of representation (and this aspect must be kept in mind, and is not secondary) compared to the blocked capilists. Finally, what will happen in the coming months is very important, from here to the referendum. I have in mind an editorial by Professor Ainis on the "Corriere della Sera" of 14 January last, in which I found myself, as arguments, with respect to my evaluations. Professor Ainis said that measures are needed to speed up and improve the implementation of the approaches contained in the reform. He quoted different ones, but among the first there was the law for the election of senator councilors. Some of us have already made proposals (the first signatory is Mr Fornaro) for the electoral law to give concrete form to the fact that the citizens will choose and designate future senators. If this, or other proposals that may be, are brought to life immediately and the proposals are framed (somehow, more cannot be done, but there can be incardination and confrontation), if this situation is already in place during the referendum and if, as I think, the referendum will approve the reform, the commitment to implement the reform will be effective and rapid. I do conclude, Madam President. I say to some of my colleagues with whom I have made some road traits (for example, Mr Micheloni, whose speech I heard this morning), that on our journey towards reform of the Constitution positions have interwoven, united, sometimes divided, as is normal, because each of us refers to our own convictions. I think that between us, not only within the PD, but between us senators and parliamentarians, first of all there must be respect, all the more so - I would say - when there is a legitimate dissent. Faced with passages of such great importance, even more so for everyone, the theme arises, which is not simple and which is also recalled in texts of political culture, of the relationship between the ethics of values, that is, their ideal convictions, and the ethics of responsibility, that is, the points of balance, listening, research, patient construction of convergences and - if you want - compromises, that are not blocked by personal absolutes, because this allows us to make decisions, to make choices that maintain consistency with the principles, those indispensable, that guide us. If you do not make this effort, you can remain either in the noble, serious witness, but that you do not build concrete works and solutions, or you can fall into a pragmatism without ideals, which can also become cynical. There is, of course, no automatism that clearly marks where the ground is, the boundary on which the ethics of values and that of responsibility meet. Everyone to define this ground must follow his own reflection, his own conscience, assume the duty of a choice of which to respond. For me personally this border and this ground for meeting in the reform has been realized with the innovations that have been introduced and that I have recalled. I respect those who do not believe that they are sufficient or satisfactory, if on the substance they are arguing and on the merits; for me they make the reform project able to be supported; then the commitment must be to implement, after the referendum, the fundamental innovations contained in it. That is the commitment and conviction that accompanies my positive vote. [[Applause from the PD Group]]

The first fundamental objection is that declared by some constitutionalists (and repeatedly reiterated in this House during the course of the process), who believe that, after the judgment n. l of 2014 of the Constitutional Court, this Parliament is not fit to undertake such a demanding reform process. In democracy the sovereignty is of the people and not of the PD assembly; it is the people who elect their representatives. With the abolition of the direct election of the representatives of the citizens in the Senate of the Republic, it will in effect be a long step backwards in time, precisely when the passive electorate belonged to few and was reserved only to those who met certain requirements of power. And that's what you want. In the reform project, proposed by a government chaired by an appointed, the members of the Senate will be chosen on the basis of a hybrid mechanism, between the accumulation of posts and the restricted elections, where the voters in practice are only the holders of other important public political positions (unions and regional councillors). Another grain, another hybrid that you have produced in the text, is precisely Article 116 of the Constitution, contained in Title V. The regions may see themselves donated by the State, other forms and special conditions of autonomy, on justice, education, tourism and cultural goods, but provided that they are in a position of balance between revenue and expenditure. Even here you have bypassed the obstacle and have not really addressed the issue. With regard to the field of work, we did not want to learn anything from the lesson given us by the reform of Title V. In fact, the need for an organic structuring of active labour policies clashes, again this time, with the fragmentations that this reform will cause (semi-reform). Ensuring that active labour policies can be delegated to regional competence will, once again, lead to an even greater increase in regional disparities. There is a need, especially in this case, for a common plan both for the organization of offices and for the planning of labour policies; only in this way will we finally be able to aim for convergence between regions and territories. Abolish perfect bicameralism means irreversibly altering Parliament's legislative capacity; and you will be responsible for it. The current government explains that by eliminating bicameralism there would be considerable savings for the state's coffers. However, this saving, which is far from significant, would only be for the functions of the Senate and not for its costs, which in fact remain almost the same. Its abolition will affect the democratic development of our country. The prediction of the constituent Fathers of the bicameralism, in fact, was expressed precisely for fear of a possible future authoritarian turn of the institutions. We risk bringing Italy, paradoxically, into a situation of institutional crisis that has never been seen before. There is always reference to countries such as France and Great Britain, both for constitutional reforms, and for other types of reforms, such as countries to be taken for example for parliamentary efficiency. Well, it is these countries, cradles of modern parliamentarism, that traditionally of democratic institutions (the first birthplace of the modern rule of law; the second cradle of parliamentarism) can afford a bicameralism that is not equal. Our country is still not ready for such a big and abrupt step. The spectre of a new authoritarianism still wanders in the rooms and places of this still bitter democracy, which has not been able to apply the articles of the Constitution in more than sixty years since its birth. It is before everyone's eyes, among other things, how corruption, favoritism and clientele are still well rooted and present in our country. Ensuring direct representation and control on the part of the citizen, who you want to make less and less part of the country's life, is the fundamental principle on which the foundation of the Movement rests. We do not need a debate on reforms that this Parliament cannot do. The law on citizenship income was framed in the Commission more than a year ago. We still do not know how long it is, because the priorities of this Government and this Parliament are others. This shows how much Parliament is committed to crucial issues, to protecting citizens. It is 1.5 million poor children and one in six Italians! ParlaMint-IT_2016-01-20-LEG17-Senato-sed-563.ana.u48	He's enlisted to speak Senator Mineo. You have the right to do so.
ParlaMint-IT_2016-01-20-LEG17-Senato-sed-563.ana.u49	Madam President, Senators and Senators, I think that during the third examination by the Senate of this draft law, we should not repeat the passions and arguments expressed first in the Commission and then in the course of the first reading, because, at this point, the constitutional ratio is not to repeat whether we agree or not, but to try to understand whether, despite everything, the reform that has been made can serve and can usefully fit into the constitutional structure. I try to alienate myself, as Ettore Scola would have said, who professionally dealt with these attempts to look things out of himself, to set up a certain type of relationship and story. The first thing I notice from the text is that, as Ainis says, on many decisive aspects the reform does not decide, or rather refers the decision to the law that will come: it is an unfinished reform. If I had to explain to college students, or to an audience of television viewers of an in-depth program, how and when the next senators will be chosen, I wouldn't know how to respond, I would stutter. I wonder what it means to say, as written in an article in the draft Constitutional Reform Act, that they will be elected by the Regional Councils and, as written in another article, that they will be chosen directly by the voters at the time of the vote of the Regional Council. It all depends on the law that comes. The reform is unfinished and no one knows when the law will be passed that will make it complete. Ainis also notes, in that article of the "Corriere della Sera" (Corriere della Sera) which, in my opinion, was improperly quoted by his friend Vannino Chiti, that for the election of the Regional Councils there was an ultimative constraint of one year in the Constitution, but the election of the Regional Councils that was to be held in 1948, as you all know, was then made in 1970. So we do not know from whom and how the new senators will be chosen, nor when they will be chosen with the new rite. Secondly, the law that we will submit to the referendum is contradictory, because on the one hand it transforms the Senate of the Republic (which will always be called so) into a Senate of the autonomouss, bringing mayors and regional councillors to Rome; on the other hand, the bill under consideration is the protagonist of an emptying of the autonomous powers of the Regions, so much so as to make an emblem of moderation, such as Senator Casini, that there is a centralist tension when the draft law introduces the clause of supremacy on the Regions in favor of the Government. There is, however, much more. When we eliminate competing legislation, dear senators, it really takes away the ambition, the possibility of contributing to national legislation and is held in a enclosure. If they are then in a fence, what is the point of turning the Upper House into a Chamber of Autonomouss? There is an implicit contradiction. The third issue that strikes me a great deal is that it is an unpredictable draft law, because it affects one of the central problems of the balance between the powers established by the Constitution in a gruff and political way: I am referring to the question of the President of the Republic. Since of course there is a relationship between the constitutional reform bill and Italicum, Ainis always notes that the Premier will be decided by the majority prize and the President of the Republic expressly gives (Article 88) the power to dissolve the Senate, but implicitly also the House, with a majority party (due to the effect of Italicum) whose leader will be able to decree the life and death of the legislature. Not only does the President of the Republic lose the power to dissolve the Senate and implicitly also the Chamber, but, as has been noted at second reading, he is elected by a procedure that lacks a closing clause. In other words, in order to prevent one from saying (this is what I call politicantism) that the Premier chose him, the President of the Republic is elected with a qualified quorum and with many votes. However, as a result of Italicum and what happens next, if the minorities were compact, we would see indecorous performances such as those of the election (or not election) of the judges of the Constitutional Court, because it will be very difficult for the Premier's candidate to pass; instead, if the oppositions eventually yielded and passed the Premier's candidate, probably the election of the President of the Republic would become the subject of a political exchange. Now, the closing clause in the majority systems exists, I mentioned it during the debate at second reading and I still remember it today. In Greece, for example, if the qualified quorum is not reached three times, the House falls, Parliament is dissolved and the vote is taken. We did not want to do this, keeping the confusion on this issue, and that makes me say that the reform is also unpredictable. For these three reasons, without any particular state of mind, I shall vote 'no' to the bill when we examine it. However, I would not just like to talk about the institutional aspect of what we are approving, but also about the political aspect, which is very important. Yesterday I followed from home, streaming, the debate in the Chamber until late at night and the impression that I had of it is painful: why did we have to do the night sitting? Just because someone said we had to vote on 20, not 21, not 22? There was no sense, no calendar obligation, just a choice, ipse ixit, you have to do it. But this, ladies and gentlemen, hides, and you all know it, a very serious political difficulty for the Premier and our Government, which today is in difficulty in its relationship with Europe. In fact, for wanting to take off the role of the diligent student who had, in a first phase, with Angela Merkel and Draghi, when he called him in the summer of 2014 (the meeting from which the job act started), and to ride the dissatisfaction widespread in many layers of the population towards Europe, the Premier made unforgivable mistakes that will pay the entire country. First mistake: while with one like Angela Merkel you can legitimately say: I don't agree with you and I seek alliances to change European policy.What can't be done, and Renzi did, is to use Kaczynski and Orban to make her a joke by voting against the Nord Stream pipeline, except then meet Putin and maybe talk about Italian orders. This kind of attitude - one that has had to be followed by European policy tells you - gives rise to reactions of no small moment. I imagine Angela Merkel will never say anything, never a word to our Premier and our Government; but she has put the cross on us, and others speak for her. The same goes for Dragons. As you know, Draghi is a political protagonist of the new Europe, not only the technical president of the European Central Bank and a Dragons who is engaged in a very difficult battle with the Bundesbank and certain financial circles that do not share, for example, quantitative easing, that is, its monetary policy, our position that does not face, as Tsipras tried to face, the problem of balance of budgets but that demands for itself, from time to time, communicating it more to the press than to formal meetings, safeguard clauses and advantages and favors, is a position that deeply breaks the boxes. From that point of view too, let us expect problems. This also explains the scandal that generated the position of Federica Mogherini, of which I mean that she was named perhaps without proper preparation, but that she worked well, and was praised by Lavrov and Kerry - who are not really the last ones - on the story of Iran. Federica Mogherini immediately got rid of the position of the Italian Government and of course the nightmare, as the newspapers write, is that a saucer such as the one that was packaged for Premier Silvio Berlusconi is prepared in Europe. I, for a criticism of European economic policy, believe that this criticism must be done loyally, by building alliances. If you use this pattern of flattering and asphalting in Europe, there are very serious problems. The reason why we have to humiliate this Senate and make it vote when the government decides without discussion is a way of hiding this profound difficulty. Then there is another difficulty, and I mean it. In these hours pressure is being put on the majority of the government and the leaders to vote in favour of the reform even if they are not convinced because, they say, otherwise the votes of Verdini would become decisive, fundamental. Verdini said: "I am Renzi's plumber. The taps of the majority on the right and on the left lose continuously; fortunately there is him repairing the tube. Gentlemen, I address my colleagues with passion: I have been a journalist all my life and there is nothing worse than self-censorship. It is much better that the law passes with 161 votes with the contribution of Verdini, rather than that you are self-censored by changing your positions, because in this way you open a drift that you know where it begins and you do not know where it ends. Third and last thing: the referendum. The election campaign for the referendum has already started and it is not about the Constitution, nor the constitutional change, but about all the things that our Government and our President-in-Office of the Council have done. It is an electoral campaign that, in a transparent way, wants to solve a gap of legitimacy that Renzi has: it has been voted by many to the primaries of the PD, then at the first trials the Democratic Party had a magnificent result in the European elections, but this Premier a legitimacy own does not have it. Having changed everything, he is the first - because he is a particularly intelligent man - to understand that he needs legitimacy. But instead of doing what Tsipras did, which has very great difficulties (today he is passing through a very complicated pension reform, then there is the problem of immigration, which affects him as much as we and much more) and which has submitted to a popular referendum on a central issue and then went to elections, here is a referendum confirming a completely improper role. They are lies, because in this country there is above all a government deficit: it is not that there are no laws to drive out those who stamp the card and leave; we are not able because we have bad administration, because we have not managed our managers, because we have a government gap that has been going on for forty years. I have never said and others have never said that we run an authoritarian risk in our country, because we have all understood that the risk we run is to continue as before, that is, with an exhausting corporate mediation. Only that the places of this mediation, instead of being those that are in it (the Parliament), are elsewhere. This is the danger: the places of mediation are elsewhere. Today, instead of being "conditioned" in the Commissions, the laws are "conditioned" by the legislative office of the Government, directed by the former chief of the police of Florence, Manzione. This is why not only the Verdini come back, but also Carboni: professionals, facilitators, creators of structures in which we average what no longer finds its place of mediation in its own roles. ParlaMint-IT_2016-01-20-LEG17-Senato-sed-563.ana.u50	Senator Bertorotta is registered to speak. You have the right to do so.
ParlaMint-IT_2016-01-20-LEG17-Senato-sed-563.ana.u51	Madam President, at this point in the debate it is really difficult to talk about reforms without being repetitive. After all, since Prime Minister Renzi adapted to the most important chair, that of Palazzo Chigi, we have been discussing it in a more assiduous way. After all, the whole game of this Executive is about this theme. I still remember the words spoken in March last year by the President himself, about the possibility that the reform does not pass: not only does the government fall, I play my whole political history. Do you believe that? I really don't. I believe that the referendum will show Renzi what the Italians really think about constitutional reform. All Italians of common sense care about our Constitution, they know that it is the result of the sacrifices of their grandparents, those who fought in war. These people know that you are trying to cancel the fundamental Charter of their civil and political rights, the basis of this country's democratic life, and I think they will not allow it to happen. You're not realizing the harm you're doing to Italy. You are not realizing that by giving way to Renzi, you will give way to the total failure of this beautiful country. How sad the dullness! Selling for a few money, selling the soul to the devil, knowing it was wrong. I would like to make a good use of the time I have been given. That is why I am going to reiterate the critical remarks that have been made by many authoritative voices. The text of the constitutional bill is not definitively established and the serious points of disagreement correspond to other not less essential ones, on which the famous pact of the Nazarene before was sure, while today it seems to have been overcome by the pact Renzi-Verdini. The need to change the structure of bicameralism is superficially shared by you by the majority, not so much for intellectual autonomy, but for a Diktat dropped from above, as you share the idea of overcoming the perfect bicameralism perhaps because you are sure to sit on the armchairs of Montecitorio, convinced that, leaving the Chamber alone the power to give confidence to the Government, and to revoke it, you will solve the problem. Perhaps you have in mind that, with this reform, by giving the new Senate the representation of territorial autonomy, the democratic system will still stand. But I want to ask you one thing: how should a Senate be built to represent the view of the self-governments? Whose expression will it really be: of citizens or their governments? The Senate will represent the territorial institutions, but not even the shadow of the people. This becomes the justification for the choice of having senators elected by regional institutions rather than by the people of each region. On the other hand, a bizarre choice for the way in which this election takes place: the regional councillors designate who of them should be elected, so everything remains within each Council, with the only addition of some poor mayor. In fact, for those who have drawn up this grandiose project it will not have been easy to ignore the Municipalities, considered their ancient roots in the country. I can imagine some mayor of my region, who will be in fibrillation right now: he can't wait to sit on the scrambles of Palazzo Madama. Yet, the real and serious problem has come to light for a few. Do you not think that these mayors-senators, like the regional councillors, will find it difficult to play a double role? Courses and appeals, double roles, double assignments. Moreover, Italy is the country of the Mastrapasqua, which can carry out as many as 25 assignments at the same time. Another uncertain issue concerns the functions to be assigned to the Senate. In a true democracy, exercising the highest constitutional functions is allowed only to those with popular legitimacy, and instead, a Senate so badly constructed even participates in the legislative function of the highest level, the revision of the Constitution, with the same powers as the elective Chamber. He is even called to elect two judges of the Constitutional Court, thus acquiring a far more decisive power than that of the present Senate. The reform assigns the election of three judges to the Chamber of Deputies (where there are more than six hundred deputies) and two judges to the new Senate, composed of only one hundred members. The power gap between the two Chambers and their members is as obvious as it is unjustified: only if the new Senate were conceived as a guarantee body, as provided for in some amending proposals, could such power be justified, but it is clear that in the established composition it is completely unthinkable to consider senators as a guarantee. After all, who will consider a mayor or a regional councilor to be a senator? I don't think anyone. The decisive role of the secretariats of the parties on their election (or, rather, on their appointment) certainly does not allow it and the function attributed to them can only appear a device to put the hands, indirectly, on the Constitutional Court, i.e. on the composition of the body that has the high task of ensuring the respect of our Constitutional Charter. Moreover, two judges at the disposal of politicians can shift the delicate balance of the Constitutional Court and also make a law clearly and fundamentally unconstitutional a constitutionally oriented law. The underlying political crux - that is the democratic representativeness of Parliament, if not even the fate of the sovereign people - emerges more clearly looking at the whole of the reforms, as we have supported, namely looking at the reform of the Senate and the new electoral law together. I would remind you that it is an approved law with clear procedural constraints and, in fact, without a real comparison, which distorts the will of voters by awarding a large prize and can allow a small minority to take over all the institutions, including the guarantee institutions. I speak of a small minority because the 40% threshold required to obtain the prize is only a misleading screen: if no party achieves the result it is not, as required by the so-called scam law of 1953, that no coalition (other essential difference) enjoys the prize and each gets the seats corresponding to the votes obtained. With the current law, the two most voted parties participate anyway in the ballot, whatever percentage they have reached, and one of the two will necessarily exceed the other, obtaining the prize in seats and domination over all, although having a very low electoral consensus. Without a threshold to participate in the ballot and the possibility to join forces, a single party takes everything, in the name of stability, governability and speed in deciding, but the stability artificially produced by electoral mechanisms created to tacitize dissent and hide social fractures only serves to take us out of constitutional democracy. You are about to vote on a measure that cancels the voices, but certainly not the fractures, while it is the gap between people and between the social groups that puts the stability of the political system at risk. It is this gap that should be bridged, as the current Constitution requires. By throwing the money, citizens no longer elect the Senate and in the election of the Chamber of Deputies their will is distorted and has very little weight. In short, from this unfortunate reform process the people come out without a voice and defeat democracy. The only hope that we in the 5 Star Movement have is in the referendum tool, which will allow citizens to express their voice. Participatory democracy is the only way to give concrete answers to the needs of communities. We have always said that democratic coexistence is structured in the close dialogue between the moment of participation and that of decision-making responsibility. Surely it is not structured in a soft dictatorship that silences the voice of ordinary citizens. We cannot call democracy the one that gives power only to one person, least of all to a President-in-Office of the Council who is not elected by the citizens. Participation and responsibility are not on different sides of the same river: participation is also the responsibility of those who have the mandate and the duty to make decisions and responsibility invests those who participate far beyond the individual electoral moment. Democracy in the absence of participation is that confined to a political technique, as a balance of powers, as an instrument of consensus and as a method, among others, to decide who must decide. You had the task of strengthening democratic institutions to face the difficult challenges of our times, those challenges you are not worrying about, challenges that have a name and surname, and they are the economic crisis, the political crisis, the social crisis and the moral crisis. We expected the Government to grasp the present and future potential of a Constitution that is and remains a living and vital norm and, instead, you have done nothing but destroy it, you have torn it apart as you have torn the hearts of the Italians who have always put trust in its 139 articles. We hope that the leader of this government, who stands as the champion of democracy, will obtain all the disapproval of the Italian people, disapproval that will certainly be expressed at the next constitutional referendum, which will finally put an end to the political life of Matteo Renzi and all those who so far have supported him, forgetting their fathers, their grandparents and even their children and grandchildren. I would personally include in the Constitution an obligation to approve at least 10% of the amendments tabled by the oppositions. So there would be a real confrontation and opposition would make sense. This Parliament, as it is set up now, is - in my view - useless. It would be the same to leave only the Government: the result would not change. We wouldn't change a comma what's happening. What we're doing is wasted time, wasted public money. That is what we are doing. [[Applause from the M5S Group]]

ParlaMint-IT_2016-01-20-LEG17-Senato-sed-563.ana.u62	Madam President, I do not intend to reply to the words of the senator who has just finished his speech. It's not worth it. With regard to this matter, I would say that we should lay out a pitiful veil and go further, because the debate is important, it concerns the Constitutional Charter. And I sincerely regret that it is treated by the Government and the majority as a mere formality, given the presence of the senators in the Chamber over the last two days, in which we have been given the opportunity to deepen the measure under consideration. And it cannot be said that this is a formality because we are at second reading and all the work that was to be done has already been done in the previous passages. If the constituent Fathers have foreseen a double reading for the changes to the Constitutional Charter, each passage has equal dignity and must represent a part of a fundamental path, before which Parliament had to be aware of the changes that had to be made. This reform, whose title mentions the repeal of the CNEL and the modification of Title V, does not really affect these issues, nor, let alone, only a modification of the composition or representation of the Senate or even perhaps, the strengthening of the power of the Executive. This reform has a very different value, much deeper and - in our view - much more serious. In fact, this reform, associated with electoral reform on the composition of the House of Deputies, breaks a balance between the powers of the State, which is its founding element. And it breaks it with an operation designed at the table, which is not accidental and has been studied not by the parliamentary composition of this House and by the various political forces represented here, but by a small group of people, namely the Council of Ministers and perhaps even more so, it was intended exclusively by the President of the Council of Ministers. This is the constitutional reform not of Parliament, but of President Renzi. There is a clear centralization of powers. There is a break in the balance between the powers of the State: in fact we will have the President of a party who will point out himself as President of the Council and will have the power to influence the list of the elected, to which he will ask to vote the President of the Republic and the members of the Constitutional Court and the MSM. I wonder if this is not a concentration of powers and I believe that those who deny it are in bad faith or want to deny the truth to themselves, in order to justify their vote in favour of this measure. I am sure that if the MPs of the Democratic Party had been faced with such a measure, with inverted parts, that is, with a different majority, they would certainly have torn their clothes, they would have shouted at the centralization of powers, which could have been identified in a form of regime and dictatorship, and would have organized various demonstrations. This hypocrisy is intolerable and makes the parliamentary path towards which we are moving even more odious. The reforms you are pursuing are, in fact, driving citizens away from the important choices that affect the life of the country. You have done so with the electoral laws and you are doing so with the reform under consideration. And I would like to make a brief reference to the electoral law for the election of representatives of the House of Deputies. You have introduced legislation that has worsened the previous electoral system, which had been launched by the centre-right, but, in fact, with the agreement of the opposition at the time. With the electoral law in force, a further principle has been introduced compared to blocked lists, which gives voters the illusion of being able to express a preference. This preference, however, will have a value in itself and will lead to the election of representatives only for the majority list that will have won the elections, which will elect candidates both among the blocked leaders of the 100 colleges, and among those who have received the preferences. On the other hand, there is the certainty for all other parties that preferences will be worth nothing, so we will have candidates voted by tens of thousands of voters who will not be elected, because with this system, for all lists other than the winner, only the leaders indicated by the parties will be elected. This, then, is a system worse than the previous one, because it deludes the voter that his vote of preference serves something. It is, therefore, a further deterioration. We are therefore strongly critical of this measure, because it is a very different concept of democracy from ours. Probably Renzi believes that the people should not be represented, but governed, commanded and needed tools to do so, and the reform you are about to approve goes precisely in this direction. I believe, moreover, that the stage of economic uncertainty and towards the institutions that we are experiencing is the fertile ground on which Renzi tries to affirm this will. It is clear that, in the face of economic crises, there is a danger that citizens will depart from political representation, believing that nothing can change and that all parties and politicians are equal. It is therefore easier to create a mechanism by which they accept amendments to the Constitution that reduce the spaces of democracy: a mechanism is created according to which, rather than a sick democracy that is unable to decide, it is preferable to rely on someone who makes decisions and commands. And we are experiencing this very situation and it is on this ground that Renzi works and works to obtain consensus with respect to the reform under consideration. A clear danger we have tried to represent in all the fora, but it has encountered the substantial disinterest and the lack of answers from the Democratic Party. The opposition, from all sides (from the 5 Star Movement to Forza Italia, from the North League to SEL), has moved the same objections, but has always remained unheard. It is not, therefore, true that there has been no debate and no requests for amendment have been made: they have been raised and have been expressed several times and more than clearly, but there has never been a responsible attitude on the part of the Democratic Party compared to all. We are moving towards a system that will lead not only one man, but certainly a small group of people that will affect the lives of all the citizens of our communities, and this danger is evident. It is creating that sort of oligarchy in front of which the citizen will no longer have the opportunity to influence and make choices on his own future. And this is the risk that we highlight and that is even clearer than the situation we are experiencing. Honestly, I am surprised that a party that refers to the tradition of the founding fathers of this Constitution, and therefore to Christian Democracy and the Communist Party, can approve an amendment like the one under consideration. Those parties, after the fascist regime and the Second World War, approved a balanced constitutional text, which could no longer pose a danger to democracy in our country. Today the Democratic Party refers to that tradition, but it does exactly the opposite, endangering the balance between powers and going in a direction that today can be entrusted to the hands of Renzi, but one tomorrow to those of anyone else will assume the power in our country. And this danger is not perceived by public opinion, nor even here in Parliament, but it is real. Honestly, I wondered whether there was an excess of concern, rhetoric and alarm on the part of the opposition forces in the past debate; whether there was an excessive game of the parties, according to which everything that the counterpart does is wrong and the concerns of the oppositions are unmotivated. I wondered about that doubt and I believe that this danger is real. Every time there are limits to democratic power, it is probably done because there is no real perception of what is happening; otherwise in the past, in most cases, changes would never have happened. I believe that we are underestimating the changes to the Constitution that we are about to vote on, and we are doing so in Parliament and public opinion is doing so outside. We start from a very trivial concept: the Constitution is dated, we need to modify it and entrust more powers to those who manage the public thing, so let's start along this path and hopefully move forward. I believe that it is wrong and dangerous and that is why we all participate in the debate: it must remain a trace, at least in the memory of the community not only of today but also of the near future, of the fact that this reform has found in the Chambers of the Senate and the Chamber of Deputies some representatives of the citizens who have highlighted the danger, the risk and have tried, within the limits of their possibilities, with the amending actions, with the words and interventions carried out in these fora, to make those who have the task of taking decisions reflect. We have done so and I think we can say that we have a clear conscience. I do not think, however, that we can say the same above all of those Members of the majority who have criticized the measure several times, that I believe they are aware of its shortcomings, but have finally, after a few skirmishes, entrenched behind a self-absolution, starting from the consideration of having done what they could, of having tried to change the text and of having obtained small and little significant changes. And, therefore, they have done their duty. In fact, those Members are perhaps more responsible than they are now than those who do not realize the seriousness of what this text contains. And perhaps they have greater responsibilities than those who, consciously, approve a constitutional text that reduces the powers of the people and of Parliament and focuses them all in the hands of a few people, and in particular those of the President-in-Office of the Council. These people, Renzi in particular, have a precise plan, they are convinced and they know what they are doing, but even those who have opposed it and who, in the end, have given up arms, have surrendered and probably in this House will even vote in favour of this constitutional reform which is absolutely wrong and dangerous for our country. On them will fall the responsibility for such a choice, and especially on them, because they were and are aware of what we are doing. And it is even more serious that the President-in-Office of the Council says that, on the referendum that will inevitably follow the adoption of this reform, he will put a question of confidence in his work. And he will not have a referendum debate on the merits of the change that will be brought to our Constitution, but a sort of plebiscite referendum on his own work, on his own person. And this is not a democratic country method, but a mentality of figures that have nothing to do with democracy. It seems a bit like what Presidents of the Council of other countries have done in the past, where the level of democracy I believe is different. Similar speeches have been made by Chávez in Venezuela and Erdoğan in Turkey and I do not know if they are the model of President of the Council and of democracy to which Renzi refers. Perhaps yes and this - in my opinion - is not a properly democratic conception of the political debate. That this concept exists in Renzi also highlights the alleged name of the party that will be born after the parliamentary experience and that will see him as leader: the Party of the Nation. Even in this case, almost the will and the presumption of being able to create a party that represents the whole nation, every political part and every citizen is highlighted. This also accentuates in us the conviction that the relationship between Renzi and democracy is quite questionable, which certainly does not find our sharing. It is also worrying that the text that will come out of Parliament reflects, in many respects, texts that have been born in other fora, which have been born in the debates of forces that are not properly democratic. And Freemasonry has been mentioned several times, and I believe not to disprove it. We have read all the texts that came out of those circles, which reflect, almost in a pedissequous or very similar way, the text that will come out of this Parliament. There is a relevance in things and not just in our words and concerns. I would like to recall here a sentence made by the President of the Republic of Naples when he arrived in this Chamber as a senator emeritus. He said that, in the face of the world crisis, a new world order was needed. This is also a worrying expression, which we do not share, which seems to highlight a renunciation of the principles of democracy; a renunciation which comes at a difficult time, as if in the face of the crises we are experiencing, which have not ended but will become more serious and will accentuate the malaise and difficulties in which our citizens will find themselves, it serves not a self-reform, a questioning of power, of those who have governed public affairs in recent decades, but an opposite reaction, aimed at concentrating powers and having more strength in suppressing the probable dissent of citizens and people; it serves to be ready, with extraordinary powers, to prevent dissent from being manifested. The feeling that we live is precisely this and it is absolutely dangerous, and we look at it with great concern. In the face of a crisis that is seriously affecting the quality of life of our citizens, which sees a concentration of wealth in the hands of the few and growing poverty, what is the state's reaction? PRESIDENT. - The debate is closed. You have to finish, Senator. TOSATO (LN-Aut). To remove power from local authorities, to remove the right to vote from citizens, to concentrate power in the hands of a few in order to be able to command and govern the people. We do not recognize ourselves in this design, we do not recognize ourselves at all, and it is clear, therefore, that our battle will continue also in the referendum campaign that will see us engaged in the coming months. ParlaMint-IT_2016-01-20-LEG17-Senato-sed-563.ana.u65	He's enlisted to speak Senator Bocchino. You have the right to do so.
ParlaMint-IT_2016-01-20-LEG17-Senato-sed-563.ana.u66	Madam President, the debate on constitutional reform, which has already begun in the country, is polluted, because our President-in-Office of the Council has spoken heavily in it, linking its political fate to the outcome of the constitutional referendum. In his narration, Renzi plans a match between two teams: innovators and immobilists, those who do not want change; a match between those who want to modernise the country and those who want to preserve it and want to prey on ancient regiments. This is the kind of debate that he is forcing into public opinion. Well, I don't feel nostalgic at all without if and without but. And in order to disassemble this kind of narrative and to be able to present myself in some way not only to the citizens, but also to the media and the general public, outside of this scheme, I want to spend the minutes at my last speech on constitutional reform trying to analyze the real and supposed reasons for the reform. Why are we proceeding with this reform? And above all, Madam President, why should we carry out a reform? I have chosen, among the many things that I have read in recent days, a step in the speech of Professor Azzariti during the Assembly held in the Chamber of Deputies on 11 January for the presentation of the Committee on No to the Constitutional referendum, a committee I am proud to have given accession. Professor Azzariti writes that two are the issues to be put at the centre of the debate. On the one hand, the issue of the crisis of the role of Parliament, deprived of its essence and its value; on the other hand, the problem of political representation, emptied of the increasingly worrying distance between rulers and rulers. The question to be asked then is: can constitutional reform reverse the course, give new impetus to the two issues on which pluralist democracy is based, or does it continue to keep us in the swamp? Professor Azzariti continues and analyses the first of the two issues: "Let's start with Parliament," he says. The perfect bicameralism has changed. Good. But do you really think - or do you want to make people believe - that the evils of parliamentarism can be dealt with by passing from perfect bicameralism to a confused bicameralism as it is what has been imagined? And here, of course, he refers to the legislative processes envisaged by the reform, which become, by one, even ten. And he goes on to say: Can one really delude ourselves that the crisis of the parliamentary system can only be tackled by intervening on the redistribution of functions and the composition of the two Chambers, not considering at all the structural reasons that are the basis of the emptying of parliamentary power? Let us try to understand the structural reasons for emptying parliamentary power. Well, Madam President, just to understand these structural reasons, I have collected data: data on the number of laws, data on their time to stay in Parliament before being promulgated. By the way, for training, I am a researcher, a scientist, and so for me the data is fundamental. If I have to analyze a problem and get the solutions, I have to start with the data, otherwise I don't understand anything and I'm just smashing and disgusting. Let's start with the number of laws in the various legislatures. Well, in the 16th legislature, the Italian Parliament passed 391 laws, of which 71 in 2011 and 102 in 2012. These are data I got from the Senate Studies Department. In order to make a comparison with other parliaments, because it is clear that we must also relate to realities present in other countries, the German Parliament passed 153 laws in 2011 and 128 in 2012, in a system where, care is taken, often we intervene with law for punctual interventions of normative maintenance. Therefore, it is a number comparable to that of the Italian Parliament. The French Parliament passed 111 laws in 2011, 82 in 2012. So, once again, they are comparable numbers, even smaller than the Italian ones. The Spanish Parliament passed only 50 laws in 2011 and only 25 in 2012. The British Parliament passed 25 laws in 2011 and 23 in 2012. A first figure jumps right to the eye. This perfect bicameralism, so much accused of being an impediment to the functioning of Parliament, is finally discovered, numbers in the hand, being now absolutely functional, in its role as legislator. The number of laws passed is exactly in line with those of other parliaments and, indeed, in some cases, even greater. On the famous shuttles, it has been said that the laws move like in a game of ping pong, going back and forth from the Chambers. Once again, numbers can help us to get a picture a little clearer than it actually does. Let us always refer to the 16th legislature (2008-2013) which, I remember, saw two governments: the Berlusconi IV Government and the Monti I Government. Well, of the 391 laws passed by that Parliament, 301 had only the two readings planned. Almost 80 percent of the laws did not require a shuttle; 75 laws required three readings. Thus, almost all of the laws have foreseen up to three readings; instead, 24 laws have had four readings. Among these laws that have had four readings there are major interventions (the provisions for economic development, the simplification of competitiveness, provisions for civil process), complex laws, which actually require four readings. Those very few who have returned more than three times to the Chambers are complex laws, so it was actually worth opening wider discussions. So for once again, Madam President, this false myth of bicameralism is denied as a proponent and promoter of ping pong and shuttles between one House and another. It's not like that. Times of law enforcement. Here is another mantra: the Italian Parliament is slow, bicameralism slows down the adoption of laws. There's some interesting data here. In the course of this legislature 188 laws were passed, in line with the 16th legislature. You think that on average, if you do the math, Parliament adopts three laws every two weeks, so it is quite efficient. Well, about the time of adoption of the laws, those of parliamentary origin in this legislature have taken an average of three hundred and ninety-two days, just over a year, to be approved. But the government bill actually only took 150 days, about five months. Here is a fundamental difference based on the origin of the laws: parliamentary laws against government laws. Of course, among the laws of government origin there are also decrees-laws: we know that these require by force of less time, sixty days. But if the latter are removed from the calculation, the laws of government origin still take two hundred and twenty days to be approved: much less than the three hundred and ninety days for laws of parliamentary origin. If we go back in time and instead consider the sixteenth legislature, we see that the time of approval of the laws of parliamentary origin was much less than in this legislature: it was about two hundred sixty days, if we do the average between the House and the Senate. So, we're talking about a little over eight months. The laws of government origin are also approved much faster - an average time of about forty days. Even if the decrees are executed once again, we have that the ordinary laws of a governmental nature were passed in the last legislature in an average time of sixty-seventy days, that is, just two months. If we go back again, to the 15th legislature, which lasted two years, that of the Prodi Government, here too the times of approval of the laws are extremely rapid: for those of parliamentary initiative only eighty days; for those of governmental origin even thirty-seven days of media: a Parliament Speedy Gonzales. If we go back again, to the 14th legislature, even here very normal approval times: the laws of parliamentary initiative took about two hundred and twenty days to be approved and those of governmental origin only forty-five days. Once again, if the decrees-laws are executed, the times of approval of the ordinary laws of government origin took only sixty days. So, ladies and gentlemen, where is the problem? Where is the problem of perfect bicameralism, if we know that the Italian Parliament is extremely efficient in law-making and can it also put absolutely reasonable times in it, how did it actually happen? What's the problem? He's somewhere else. Let us look at what Parliament is dealing with, for example, and what difference there is again between laws of government origin and laws of parliamentary origin. The government deals with economic measures, reforms, constitutional changes, foreign policy, while Parliament deals with minor issues: the establishment of committees, monuments and celebrations, ratifications of international treaties and delegations to the government. Once again, Parliament's real problem arises: this inequality, this diversity between laws of government origin and laws of parliamentary origin. A final figure, for all and above all: if we consider the percentage of approved laws of governmental origin and parliamentary origin, we come to the real core of the matter. In this parliamentary term 80% of the approved laws are of governmental origin and only 18% are of parliamentary origin. The same numbers are also evident in previous legislation. The question of trust was specifically dealt with in 30% of the laws that were passed. In particular, we have heavy assumptions of trust issues on important measures. Some measures, more significant, have required three votes of confidence: stability 2014, stability 2015, jobs act, reform of public administration and - take care - Italicum. The electoral law accompanying this constitutional reform required three votes of confidence in the House, in three different articles. In general, 30% of the approved decree-laws require a vote of confidence. Here we reach the core of the problem: the imposition of the question of trust on important measures, even on the electoral law, which has evoked, as we all know, embarrassing historical precedents (the Acerbo law, which was passed with a vote of confidence). I don't want to end up in the easy qualunquism, in demagogy, and give fascist to this or that; but I can't forget history. In the elections of 1921, carried out with a proportional system, there were coalition governments and Mussolini, who became President of the Council, devised a law in order to be able to diminish the role of Parliament. There is no authoritarian risk. My colleague, Mineo, preceded me. This time the project, as well as at the time, is to move the decisions elsewhere. Mussolini succeeded with a law that provided for proportional representation with a majority prize given to those who exceeded 25 percent of the votes. There was a great deal of discussion in the Chamber and in the Commission on this matter, in order to try to increase this threshold of the majority prize to 40% (that of the Italicum), but it was not possible at that time, because of the opposition of the Government. Well, today, ninety years later, the threshold is 40%, but the government with an escamotage, that of the ballot, has allowed to elude it. In fact, it is well known that, precisely because of the ballot and considering the reduction of the voters that most likely will occur at the round of ballots, a party with only 20% of the votes of the members on the electoral lists can actually obtain the majority, being in effect equivalent to that law Acerbo that was then, as we all know, the last electoral law to be applied in the elections. The subsequent elections, in fact, only included one ballot box with a yes and a no. Well, what is the cause of these reforms? Continue Azzariti in his speech: It is the form of parliamentary government that must be rethought, today suffering because of the imbalance in relations between the Government and Parliament. - the figures say - and we have seen it - imbalance in favour of the former and at the expense of the latter. The wise auditor, the true innovator, rather than favoring the involution by strengthening the powers of the Executive and further compressing those of the legislative, should do exactly the opposite. It is necessary to limit and regulate the government's overpower in Parliament, intervening on the unjustified flow of demands for confidence, on the urgent decree, on the maximendament, which humiliate the autonomy of Parliament and Members of Parliament; regulations should be rewritten, in order to regulate the parliamentary debate and avoid the contingent times that prevent confrontation; it would be necessary to assign to the opposition a well-defined statute and guarantee, thus hindering the obstructive practices sometimes improperly used; it is urgent to intervene on the organization of the work to redefine the relationship between the Commissions and the Chamber, relocating to the centre the Commissions - a true place of deepening and free discussion - compared to the House which now represents nothing more than a theatre of division, an empty and only spectacular representation of our parliamentary organ and our - often scalmanati - representatives. So writes Azzariti. Well, I do not want to deny the need for reform. I go even further: we discuss the perfect bicameralism. But, be careful, let us discuss this only once all these structural issues have been addressed. Given to the hand, once we have rethinked the Regulations, once we have redefined the role of the Commissions, then only a constitutional legislator, attentive to the reality of things and with the will to really solve the problems, will be able to call into question even the perfect bicameralism. To do it first, no; to do it first is suicide for democracy because we are moving once again - I repeat for the second time - the place of debates from Parliament to elsewhere and this is no longer a democracy. Madam President, I have never loved our Constitution as it is right now. I'll do a mea culpa. She, the Constitution, was there to exert her beneficial effects on our civil life; pulled with the jacket, sometimes reproached, sometimes betrayed, but she was there to regulate our civil life. And I - I confess and here lies the mea culpa - looked at her distractedly. Now, our Constitution is once again at risk. It is a real risk that I see and witness as part of this Parliament at a time when it is being put at risk. I see it; it's concrete; there's a risk. Now that it is at risk and that clumsy tampers are frightened on the horizon, because such are in the absence of a valid justification for a reform process that does not solve the problems; only now, mea culpa, I breathe deeply every syllable of our Constitution, connect the various pieces and enjoy the splendid and far-sighted design traced by those who have seen with their own eyes the horrors of the past century. Now defending her is my greatest commitment. There will be a new constituent season: when this referendum - as I hope - will fail this reform and a new constituent season will open. There will be time and time to start a debate on our Constitution again. Now and today, however, at this time, defending this Constitution is the greatest commitment. [[Applause from the Misso-SEL Group]]

ParlaMint-IT_2016-01-20-LEG17-Senato-sed-563.ana.u68	Madam President, this deformity - as I call it - does not serve to reduce the costs of politics because otherwise it would have accepted our proposals for half-time Members even in the House, which is more numerous, of half-wages. Nothing has been accepted. It does not even speed up the legislative process, otherwise a specific function would have been assigned to the Senate, so as to lift the House from some tasks and have two Chambers working in parallel without overlapping each other. Nor does it serve to give governability because the crucial node of governability are the turncoats, that is, the faculty that any elected person today has to betray the elective mandate, placing himself outside the coalition or the Group with which he was elected according to personal conveniences or mandates. So what's the point of this constitution? It serves to seal a drift that political propaganda has already instilled, passing concepts totally unrelated to our Constitution as premierate and as governor of the Region. It is helped in this, as a Trojan horse, also by the majority laws that, however, have a legitimacy when Regions and mayors have a limitation in the legislative and regulatory power. This semantic deformation, however, President, had to be surrounded at least by those components of the institutional architecture that had this specific task and that on the Constitution even in some cases swear. Because the most beautiful Constitution in the world - as it has been defined - identifies the Italian system as a parliamentary republic, that is, a system in which the representation of the popular will is entrusted to Parliament which, as elected and chosen directly by the people, is the holder of legislative action, elects the President of the Republic and legitimizes the Government, through the vote of confidence and, moreover, directs its action. With disgust, it was in these very classrooms that I was told that the formal Constitution is opposed to a Constitution in fact, the real one. These colleagues used to say it between the serious and the facet, as sometimes is done to jinx the naives. Ingenuous? None of us escaped that an unconstitutional electoral law had been promptly signed by the President of the Republic and that it was used for three elections, without anyone objecting and that they - their own, who gave us the lesson - appointed by a party secretary, embodied the betrayal of the Constitution and the popular will. We have denounced him for years, as we denounce the ad personam laws, well before the Constitutional Court judgments and before any other. Where's the ingenuity, then? No, Mr President, we cannot speak of naivety, so we cannot define knowing how to keep alive the ability to be indignant and not to give up in the face of the breakdown of things. There is no naivety in this. Piero Fassino tried to make us look illusory, when he said: make a party and let's see who votes for you. Here we are; and the more the walls of your ivory tower rise, the greater our strength and determination to defend the Constitution, bent and plagued, both in its non-application and in the revision process. Already today the Constitution is betrayed and the legislative power is usurped in various ways by the Government, sometimes in a subtle way, resorting to the right bugs - where is Senator Cociancich? - sometimes in a crude and brutal way, like good manzoni's memory. It has been recalled that 80% of the approved laws are of government initiative and how much the process of government-led billing is faster, than those of parliamentary initiative, with one exception, which confirms the rule: the so-called bill Boccaduttri, formally of parliamentary initiative, but basically wanted to cover the responsibilities of the Government, which had knowingly failed to provide the necessary resources to the committees that verified the statements of the parties, so that they were able to collect, without any control, public funding. Well, in this rancid legislative stock, the presence of innumerable abuses by the Government on the vote of confidence and the decree of urgency is extremely impressive. These are unconstitutional, irrational abuses, often imposed to correct previous errors, or completely useless, where there are then no implementing decrees, and therefore good only to exhaust the ephemeral time of advertising. All this, however, is not enough: on public appointments, from the Presidency of ISTAT to that of the Council of State, passing through the very delicate one of the Board of Directors of RAI, the Government wanted to impose its Diktat, revealing its face. What Renzi calls destruction is actually a system of eviction and personal occupation of the state's vital ganglia. Not satisfied, Matteo Renzi has centralized further powers and competences in the Presidency of the Council and forfeited resources, as we have seen in the Law Madia of Public Administration Reform. Already today, therefore, we are living in a de facto premiere. Why, then, amend the Constitution? Because what is happening today is based solely on the ignovia, the selective blindness of the righteous, who should watch and turn the head or the bow, but the system is not perfect and someone could, as happened by the electoral law or by the blocking of pensions, block the gear and reveal us that the king is naked. With this reform, this will become impossible or difficult. The Renzi-Boschi reform completes, seals and crystallizes the path marked in recent years, giving the majority party all powers. It is a sanatorium to the de facto occupation of a power (the executive one), the legislative power and the judicial one; a sanatoria that will make legal what is illegal. That is why we are talking about honesty, because legality is now empty, it is even a dangerous screen that hides the dangers and enemies of democracy. In other countries there are systems that favour governing democracy rather than the representation of citizens, but they have counterweights so that this power does not become overpowering. In the United States and France it is often the case that the President is an expression of a party and the parliamentary majority has a different matrix; none of this is provided for in the absolute premierate which comes from the art-made tension of the electoral law signed at the Nazarene and from this deformity of the Parliament, which gives to the Head of Government, supported by the first party, the absolute control of the majority of parliamentarians, no longer chosen by the citizens but appointed, once again appointed. We will have a reverse Constitution: no longer a Parliament that gives confidence to governments, but only one man to the command that gives (let's call it so) confidence in time to parliamentarians, one of those leashes for small dogs extending or shortenable at the discretion of the dominus. [[Applause from the M5S Group]] The legislative process will essentially be governed by the Executive, putting the House of Deputies in a corner. The marginalization even becomes humiliation where the Commission will, among others, have the power to impose on Parliament its laws, its priorities. Seventy days will be allowed to do what the Government wants: this is the so-called voting institution on a certain date. You see that in this case the mechanism of delegation and ownership of the legislative power are reversed. It's all above: how can you not feel nausea in this vertigo? As for the guarantee bodies, the President of the Republic and the Constitutional Court, according to the new mechanisms, will be the expression of the Government, rather than of a party secretary, since precisely the Democratic Party has established in its statute that these two figures, for their political strength, will have to coincide. The absolute Premier will have (see, Madam President, the result of this drift: if I myself come to say Premier, it means that we really have reached the semantic marasma) 80% of the Members, of the nominations of guarantee and will affect even more than today on the legislative process. The underlying approach behind it is not aimed at revising the Constitution, but at overcoming it. So one enters a new territory, that of simple decisions, because by law the Government and also the legislative power are attributed to one person. What about the control organs? I am concerned about the militarisation of the Constitutional Court.
