ParlaMint-IT_2016-01-20-LEG17-Senato-sed-563.ana.u75	Madam President, ladies and gentlemen, representatives of the Government, I would like to start by starting with a reflection and a paradox that we are experiencing. Never so much trust in the world is placed today in democracy. Research conducted in 57 countries (or 85% of the world's population) indicates that the world's citizens, 91.6%, identify democracy as a good way to rule their own country. It wasn't always like that. At the end of the Second World War there were only 12 democracies worthy of this name; they rose to 44 in 1972 and 72 in 1993, while today there are 117 out of 195 countries, of which 90 actual democracies. But where is the paradox? It is in the fact that precisely in the historical phase in which democracy becomes predominant, precisely when it wins over authoritarianism and totalitarianism, representative democracy is under check, it is under attack. In the main European and Western nations the urn tributaries are declining, the temptation to shortcut the strong leader grows, institutions and parties are at the lowest levels of confidence. In essence, institutions and parties are accused of being substantially useless, of not affecting, and decisions are taken elsewhere, in institutions not elected by citizens. That is why overcoming the bicameralism of equality is now a must to strengthen democracy, not to weaken it: because in the times in which we live, those of globalization, there is a need for authoritative democratic institutions, democratically legitimized and able to give answers in the times dictated by contemporaryism. Today we are at the last reading of the bill of constitutional reform for the Senate and thought can only go to the extraordinary work of the Constituents, accomplished in a short time and with extraordinary results in terms of national cohesion and strengthening and maintaining democratic institutions. That is why the referendum should and must be an opportunity for democratic confrontation of a mature country, a confrontation on the merits of the proposals for reform, and it would be wrong to transform it, I say it forcefully, on one hand and on the other, into a sort of ordain, a rustic duel, on the one hand, with apocalyptic visions and insults and, on the other, the transformation of the referendum into a pronunciation on itself. The referendum is and must remain on the reforms: the pronunciation on the government's activities, in democracy, is called elections. Today a good Constitution must find a correct and efficient balance between governance and representation; that is why overcoming the perfect bicameralism goes in the right direction. The Senate, however, must not be interpreted as a sort of after-work or be lived as an old tool of a time that was, but as a tool to make more effective both the legislative activity and the representation of the territories. At this point, the source of legitimacy of the new Senate, which must hold together a territorial legitimacy (the Regions) and the electoral body, becomes fundamental. Article 2 of the text that we are going to vote amends Article 57 of the Constitution and, in paragraph 2, provides that the Regional Councils elect the senators, while paragraph 5 specifies that this must be done in accordance with the choices expressed by the citizens. On this point we discussed and today, together with twenty-three other colleagues, we deposited a bill, provided for in paragraph 6 of the new Article 57 of the Constitution, on the rules for the election of the Senate of the Republic, in order to try to keep together the dual legitimacy of the new senators: the territorial one, being their regional advisers, and that of the citizens, respecting the choices expressed by the latter. A proposal that strengthens the new Senate, representation and the overall structure of reforms. The adoption of this bill would also be an effective response to criticism, which is not entirely unfounded, according to which the combined arrangement of the new electoral law of the House and a bad electoral law for the Senate would give a majority of parliamentarians not chosen by the citizens, but appointed. The proposal is very simple: on the day of the regional elections citizens should receive two ballots, the first for the election and renewal of the regional councils and the new President of the Region, the second for choosing the senators assigned to that Region, having first, very banally, divided the regional territory into as many colleges as the senators assigned to the Region itself. (15.45) (Follows FORNARO). In this way, by giving absolutely proportional votes, transformed into seats, to the individual lists, we would make an internal ranking on each list and the best percentage results of the colleges would represent the ranking that would basically give the names of the elected. And at the first session of the Regional Council, basically, as the Regional Council takes note of the election results for the Regional Council, it would also take note of the results of the choices made by the citizens. We expect there to be positive signs on this proposal of ours, which is an ideal continuation of the battle made by us to include that famous amendment to paragraph 5 of Article 2: positive signals, starting with the Government, because it would not be understood, I say it with great clarity to the Subsecretaries present, a stronghold on electoral models for the election of the new Senate, provided for by paragraph 6 of Article 57, or on other models, such as lists. These would be read as a camouflage, as a nomination of the new senators, with exactly the same damage produced with the Porcellum, which was one of the leaders of the fall of the representativeness of the Senate and the House and has surely increased the gap between citizens and institutions, between citizens and their representatives. I will conclude where I started: today we need authoritative, legitimate institutions capable of responding in times of contemporaneity. But constitutionalist Ainis reminded us a few days ago that every constitutional revision called a principle of reform is not the whole reform. This reform refers in decisive passages, Ainis recalls, to the ordinary law, starting precisely from the electoral matter. And on this, both in a desirable modification of the Italicum that eliminates the blocked capitalists, and by adhering to this proposal of model of election of the senators, we will give a very strong, clear and clear answer, and I think we would thus pave the way for a victory of the referendum, especially to return to the heart of the problem. The heart of the problem is precisely overcoming the perfect bicameralism. For these reasons I shall vote in favour, but I will remind myself, my party and my government, that a good constitution must be accompanied by a good electoral law. Because if a good Constitution is accompanied by a bad electoral law then it can become, in practice, a bad Constitution. That is why we, together with other colleagues and - I believe - the Italians all have the right to know in detail the electoral law for the new Senate before the referendum, in order to be able to fully express a judgment on the maximum opportunity provided by the constituents in Article 138 in the coming autumn. [[Applause from the PD Group]]

ParlaMint-IT_2016-01-20-LEG17-Senato-sed-563.ana.u77	Mr President, with some embarrassment, with great dissatisfaction, both as a citizen and as a representative of the electorate, I am returning to speak on this draft law for the reform of our Constitutional Charter. Every time this House comes back to deal with the reforms, the debate, rather than focusing on the merit of the reform itself, tends increasingly to focus on the perverse direction of the government, the inappropriate accelerations and the blind compression of every parliamentary dialectics. All these considerations confirm, once again, that for the PD and the Government it was not a question of carrying out a real draft revision of the Constitution, but we were looking for only a symbol, a spot reform to root the electoral consensus of a Government born in the incubator of the primaries for the election of the Secretary of the Democratic Party, but never legitimized by the people. Renzi and his gangherati associates, with the Nazarene first and with the Verdinian tutoring then, have dismantled the constituent season, started with fatigue in the spring of 2013 and, avoiding any confrontation with the opposition parliamentary forces, have imposed a model of armored reform, modified in the first parliamentary passages only in some marginal aspects, and complementary to the perverse electoral law passed last year. What a constituent legislature! The current government took the same path as the 2001 (Title V) and 2005 reforms, which were characterized by the unilateral imposition by the majorities of the time and by a lack of sharing with the social and political forces outside them. And the results of those attempts are well known to all of us: the reform of Title V has produced a number of negative consequences, whose effects are unfortunately destined not to be exhausted. The Berlusconi Government's attempt in 2005, indeed much more balanced than the current draft law, ended with the sinking of the 2006 referendum. And just starting from this last failure, the current Premier - aware of the deficit of participation and sharing recorded in the present reform - focuses everything on the referendum next autumn, trying to turn it into a plebiscite on its increasingly weak leadership. Constitutional reform, therefore, becomes only an electoral instrument: the Constitutional Charter transformed into a form, a spreadsheet for an electoral poll. It never happened. They change and transform important parts of our fundamental Charter only to allow the demagogic and electoral games of Renzi and his acolytes. Thus it is explained that, for a few days already, the Premier has started the referendum campaign, a period of ten months in which Italian politics will be enraged by an incandescent opposition between supporters and opponents of the reforms. Meanwhile, Italy's problems will remain in the background. The Government, committed to supporting the referendum, is already neglecting - and will do so throughout 2016 - the serious problems of our country. In the face of an unproductive and painful confrontation with Europe, the questions concerning everyday life of Italians remain unresolved. Only by way of example, the thousands of savers of the four banks remain unanswered so far, despite the government's repeated promises over the past few weeks. They are still waiting to know the citizens and workers of Taranto what will become of Ilva and if, after the failure of the decree-law (still in the process of conversion), we have another to lengthen the agony of the ionic factory. And what will happen, with regard to referendums, in all coastal regions affected by drilling: will the Descalzi-Guidi line prevail or that of the governors, with the Premier struggling to pursue even the last decisions of the Constitutional Court in the role of incapable referee, who changes tactics every week? And the many times assumed resumptions of consumption and gross domestic product? With an inert and irreconclusive government (all taken from the meetings and referendums) it will be difficult for an improvement of the economic conditions of our country to be recorded. Yet countries such as Spain and Ireland (which have experienced a much more serious crisis than ours in recent years) have reversed the trend with significantly more significant numbers. What about our maròs? And our hesitant foreign policy, which sees us passive spectators even of what happens just a few steps from home, in the southern Mediterranean? All these questions, along with many others, will unfortunately remain unanswered, since the President-in-Office of the Council will have to turn the Peninsula, from country to country, to support his referendum, his fanciful plebiscite. But this is not constitutional reform, but only an empty election campaign for the selfish affirmation of one's individual political interests. These reasons lead us to support our position against this reform. For the truth, it is a matter of repeating what we, Conservatives and Reformists, have declared since August 2014, when in the Forza Italia Group we had the courage to distance ourselves from the Nazarene Pactum Sceleris, declaring all our perplexities about the bill today at our examination. We did it almost alone, being pointed out negatively by the Roman-Green duo. The time, however, was gallant man: most of the Group of Forza Italia, beginning with President Romani himself, then aligned itself to our position, resoundingly denying the agreements contained in the Nazarene contract. Verdini, for his part, has instead dropped every veil of hypocrisy and has passed, weapons and luggage, in the compagine renziana, returning to the Grand Duchy. It is a bitter observation, but in summer 2014 we had seen right. The pact concluded by Berlusconi and Renzi was the worst shop with which we tried to give political support to a bad reform of our institutions. In addition to these considerations, the reasons for why we are opposed to this reform remain intact. On all, we repeat it again, great attention must be paid to the perverse effect that the new constitutional architecture, adding to the predictions of the new electoral law (the Italicum), will entail for our system. Those who win the elections will be able to dominate our institutions - as many colleagues have said - by directly electing or effectively influencing the highest positions of the State. In the name of a declared efficiency, the majority party will control not only Parliament (as well as it is lawful), but all the most important institutions, from the President of the Republic to the Council. I ask the PD members: with Italicum and reform of the Constitution, how many ad personam laws could be passed in the near future? Finally, I would like to reiterate here the regret for the total lack of confrontation and dialogue that has characterized this reform, which, in different circumstances, we would have liked to make our contribution. As I have been able to represent in the previous debate in October, our Group has tried to bring corrective measures to the text of the draft law, with amendments which I do not exaggerate to define quality. We have proposed that the principle of the tax ceiling be included in the text of the Constitution, i.e. the introduction of a maximum threshold of tax pressure capable of protecting the taxpayer and preventing the perverse actions of governments. We have suggested that the concept of so-called infrastructural equalization be fixed, in order to harmonize growth between the different areas of the country (the rich and evolved ones and those lagging and backward from the socio-economic point of view). Noteworthy was also the proposal to include and regulate the primaries, as a constitutional instrument for the democratic selection of the new ruling classes: quality amendments - I said - and, perhaps because little demagogic and too constructive, completely ignored by the majority. Also for these reasons, in addition to the reasons for the opportunity highlighted at the beginning of my speech, our no to the bill will once again be strong and convinced. We will continue not to bow to the weak arguments of the government and we will continue our strong opposition, with the serious and responsible style that has characterized us over the last two years, to which is opposed the harmful overtachism and the narcissistic vanagloria of the President-in-Office of the Council. ParlaMint-IT_2016-01-20-LEG17-Senato-sed-563.ana.u81	Mr President, honourable representative of the Government, fellow senators, the Senate is at the last stage of its journey. I hope, like the previous speaker, that we will not get a majority in the vote tonight, and so I hope that, in these three months of reflection, there will be many colleagues who, unlike me, have changed their views on the substance of this bill. I should therefore like to repeat what I said in my previous speech. However, I believe that, as Senator Romani said when we discussed the calendar, this could be the Senate's farewell to itself deserved a less vulgar atmosphere than the one in which it took place. In these days, we have the thunderous protagonism of a President-in-Office of the Council who is happy because he announces a return of Italy to Europe. It does not seem to me that way, and above all, it does not seem to me to operate in this sense the constitutional reform of which he boasts so much. What seems even more painful to me is that for at least twenty days, the Senate vote is taken for granted and the House vote in three months' time and a political confrontation is announced on the Constitution between the President of the Council and the rest of the country. I think that this House has a duty to say goodbye to a part of the history of Italy with much greater composure than that to which President Grasso has forced the progress, the times and the developments of our debate. The Senate, the Italian one, the royal one, the non-elective one, was born in the prediction of a disaster: it was thought that the great Sicilian and Piedmontese aristocracy would be taken away. I remember the words of the Prince of Salina: we are not interested in being senators, we are cats, others are jackals. The prince of Salina was wrong rotten: that Senate, those appointments, made at the beginning of the Kingdom, were of the first order and the place that in the history of Italy occupies the Royal Senate is more than dignified, even when it had to lend - let us think of the defeat in the war of 1866 - to the dirtier work, that of jurisdiction over Admiral Persano. Yet in the history of Italy there is an ancient anti-parliamentaryism. I do not know whether it is really anti-parliamentaryism or disappointment about the unity of Italy. Let's think of that beautiful journalistic reportage, "I moribundi del Palazzo Carignano" (the Chamber was spoken of) by Petruccelli della Gattina: a text of anti-parliamentaryism was judged for some generations. No, it wasn't like that at all. In order to remain at the playground of Sicilian literature, a much stronger anti-parliamentaryism, directed towards the Chamber and not towards the Senate, was that of a great Italian writer, Federico De Roberto, in the pages of "L'imperio" (we are in the Giolittiana age, in the second half of the nineteenth century, 1895): the novel takes place all in Montecitorio and the protagonist leans on what seems to him a reference marble and realizes that it is cardboard. This anti-parliamentaryism has often accompanied the history of Italy. Let's think of that lay revolt against Parliament, led by Gabriele D'Annunzio, against Giolitti, who brought Italy to war on 24 May 1915. Three years ago, a willing friend, Minister Quagliariello, led with expert hands by the magisterium of influence and persuasion of the then head of the State Giorgio Napolitano, said he had called in the Cabinet 42 representatives of the best Italian legal and political cultures; it may be that they were, but no one noticed. Why? Because the legal and political cultures of modern Italy had disappeared, absorbed, swallowed up and digested from mass moralism, from the perverse subculture of anti-politics. So said a colleague from the Democratic Party, Senator Micheloni, this morning. I call it mass moralism. But maybe we're all talking about the same thing. Today, then, we have to judge a path, not that of Quagliariello, but I would say a slightly longer path, what perhaps begins in the mid-1980s with the conclusions of the work of the Commission Bozzi. There may still be real and serious political and legal cultures there. Many problems were reviewed, an investigation was created, in the wake of Craxi's reformatory demands. He tried to resume that work in the message of President Cossiga of the last phase of his seven-year period. He returned to office with the bicameral commission which in 1992 had to propose together something constitutional and something for electoral legislation. Why couldn't it happen? Because square moralism, mass moralism, the hateful doga, which we had seen expressed only in the reserved spaces of the Supreme Council of the judiciary and the associated judiciary, invaded the media squares and not only. Entire political cultures were overwhelmed: the socialist, the liberal, the republican, the Christian Democrat and the Social Democrat. There is very little left of the communist political culture itself, which was intended to protect itself. In his vulgate, the prestigious colleague Napolitano tells us that at the Constituent the old communists were intransigently monocameralists, for depositum fidei democratico-jacobino, and then bent to bicameralism. But how could a six-month guarantee instrument of the decisive elections of 18 April be challenged? Then, says the prestigious colleague - and many in this House refer to him - the regional institute was born and it finally deserved parliamentary or similar representation. This is a fraud, it is a lie: the regional institute entered the Constitution with the secessionist blackmail, not of Calderoli and Bossi, but of the Sicilian oligarchy. De Gasperi had to sign a decree - and it did well, because Sicily means so much in the history of Italy - that it recognized Sicilian autonomy. But there were in the Constituent those who opposed the regional institute. A speech by Nitti in 1946 seems to have spoken of today: without a financial rule, the State of the self-government can only be on the back of the national state: public spending expanded, participated and perhaps in perspective guapperia at the gaming tables of Berlin and Brussels. The scam continued, and the Regions in 1970 were made without a financial law, in return for the national state. It was uncertain if they were to be of legislation or management. Today they are exclusively of management, and only the will of the very willing minister Quagliariello could glimpse in the new so-called Senate a Chamber of compensation that would take over the squalor of the State-Regions-Autonomous Conference. So, as you can see, the reform we are facing is wrong. Of course, the Senate comes out of the picture with no regrets. In our debate, quite rightly, there has been no gesture of homage to the parliamentaryism desired by the Constitution and to the high democratic consciences that have attended this House. There has been some uncomfortable concern about the disordered Assembly which will inherit its name and which will, in fact, be the playroom of the regional councillors. At first it seems to me that mayors prevailed in the text that the government submitted to us. In short, there is an activating regionalism without honor, nor glory in the heart of our Constitution and our budget. This House will lexically inherit the Senate name, but it will not have any title under any code of parliamentary law. As for the last debate we had here last October, it was then persecuted - I was not prejudiced against it, even if the Presidency was really unhappy in guaranteeing freedom of opinion to the senators - especially the idea of a peace between senators of the Democratic Party. Then the peace between the democratic senators seemed, that is, a public good to be protected before, more and better than the freedom of the other senators: hence the use of kangaroos, contingency times and all kinds of vulgarity on the part of those who, as President of the Senate, should have felt the honour of representing representation, and instead seemed to act as exponent of civil society, that is to offend the Senate much more than that councilman of State Montagna who in 1945 was responsible for the closure of the royal institution. Mr President, without any temptation to vote in favour, today - I hope not, but it seems to me fatal to do so - we will say 'no' to an institution of the democratic history of this country. [[Applause from the GAL Group (GS, PPI, M, MBI, Id, E-E).]]

ParlaMint-IT_2016-01-20-LEG17-Senato-sed-563.ana.u83	Mr President, Madam Minister, the long process of approval of constitutional reform is finally coming to an end. It is a reform that has great historical value, at least for the fact that it has been waiting for decades. For decades, in fact, political forces and Parliament have been discussing this without ever reaching a conclusion and without ever reaching a final approval of the many projects presented in Parliament. It may also surprise the fact that this legislature, which was born with strong political instability, has become a constituent legislature, whereas in the past Parliaments with much larger and stronger majorities had not been able to carry out solemnly announced reforms. This - it must be acknowledged - is mainly due to the acceleration of politics by the Secretary of the Democratic Party, Matteo Renzi, who later became President of the Council. This has meant that the legislature could become a legislature of institutional reforms and economic reforms, closely linked elements, which, in my opinion, are the two sides of the same coin. In fact, there can be no political stability and reformist action of the government if the institutions are not modernised and adapted to a context that, more than ever before, requires rapid and timely decisions, therefore the abolition of the perfect bicameralism and the strengthening of the executive power while respecting the powers of control of Parliament. As we know, in the right and left past they have mutually rejected the reforms proposed by others, always resetting the work undertaken by others and always requiring to start over. In this legislature, however, the agreement on reforms between the Democratic Party, between the majority and Forza Italia, had made us hope, beyond the numbers in Parliament, that this game would end and we could finally pass a new electoral law and the reform of the institutions with a pact shared in the interest of the country. Unfortunately, this pact has been denied, as we know, and in my view it has been unreasonable. There are those who, however, like the new AL-A Parliamentary Group, to which I belong, do not intend to change their mind and do not intend to change their position without a plausible and reasoned reason. And even with the contribution of AL-A, the reforms so long awaited and necessary for our country will be approved by Parliament. The opposition forces, however, are proposing to reject them in the referendum. For fairness and honesty, it must be said that there are political forces that can boast at least a coherence in this opposition. [[Comments from the M5S Group.]] I'm talking about you. But there are others that incomprehensiblely, having included in their programme the renewal of the institutions and above all having approved this reform at the first parliamentary passage and then disowning it, today they join the choir of those who want to reject it. We will thus have a front for the abolition of reforms ranging from the League to Fassina, from Forza Italia to Di Pietro and Rodotà, from Meloni to the 5 Star Movement: all together for the preservation of the existing that does not work, which is one of the causes of the failure of the Italian political system. Precisely because of these absurdities, I am convinced that to transform the referendum into a yes or a no to President Renzi - and I do not happen to say to President Renzi and not to the Government - is not the Premier but the opposition, which forms an absurd and not credible juxtaposition in order to contest Renzi's work, in the illusion of giving a backdrop to the Government regardless of the content of a reform. Well, I believe that the citizens will understand yet another deception and act of irresponsibility of certain political forces, of those who talk about change and then strongly oppose the only change that Parliament has made possible and that can allow us to get out of the paralysis of these years. So those who ask Italian citizens to vote no to the referendum to cancel a reform that finally overcomes the perfect bicameralism, will have to explain the reasons for it. Anyone who calls for a vote on a reform setting up an assembly of local self-governments, such as the completion of federalism, will have to say why. Those who ask to vote no to the revision of the notorious reform of Title V of the Constitution, which, by universal admission, has paralysed the action of national government because of an endemic conflict and an absolute confusion between the different powers of the State, will have to give explanations. Finally, those who call for the vote to be taken on a reform which simplifies the procedures for the adoption of laws, giving Parliament real centre stage, will have to take account of this wretched choice in the face of future generations. I am convinced, however, that Italians will understand the faction of this opposition that brings together political forces so distant and different, united by the sole aim of not recognizing the merit of this majority and this Government in giving an epochal turning point to the institutions. I am convinced that Italians will be able to make the best choice, the one that will make progress in Italy in terms of economic and social progress and in terms of rights and democratic civilization. And the outcome of this referendum will mark a profound renewal of Italian political life. So, Mr President, Mrs Minister, in the referendum to vote yes to the future; to vote yes will be a yes to change; to vote yes will be a yes to an Italy that wants to become a country that is finally modern, capable of facing the tough challenges facing us. ParlaMint-IT_2016-01-20-LEG17-Senato-sed-563.ana.u84	Mr President, I will also make a few comments because I have been in this House for several months now, like my colleagues in my Group, to explain the reasons why, with great conviction and with great electoral strength, we do not share and reject, together with the sovereign people, this mistaken idea of constitutional reform. In order to motivate the reasons why I think that the political operation of the majority in government in recent months has been deeply wrong, I would like to start from a fundamental reflection and contextualize the debate that we are having this evening that risks, and has always risked in these months, being presented as detached from the political context and even to the social context that we live in. First of all, after listening to my colleague, Senator Fornaro, I would like to say that I share an element of his reasoning, especially what he said in the preamble. We really live in a very particular historical phase, in which we are dealing with a sort of paradox: democracy which, fortunately, becomes the most significant form of government in many countries of the world, with an increasing level very important compared to even a few years ago. Finally, many countries on the globe take the democratic form as the only possible form of government. Inside, however, this expansion of democratic forms, there is the deep crisis of representative democracy and of course all that means. Even in our country, in fact, we have seen how stringent and penetrating this element of crisis has been. This seems to me to be a fundamental point of the debate we are holding, because I say to the Government that, in my opinion, the most significant mistake of this debate is born on this issue. We, Italy, should have, at the time we went to reform the Constitution, first of all posed this problem: before these elements of crisis, what are the most effective tools to counter them and how the trend is reversed compared to what has happened in recent years. I would like to add another element of premise, which is the other point which seems to me to have completely missed in the reflection of all these months. I believe that there is a great element of underestimation, of how the social crisis and the economic crisis that are dramatically on the one hand and the moral crisis with the loss of representativeness even of the intermediate bodies, as we have historically known, represent a potentially explosive mixture with respect to which the answers that a majority of Government that takes on to the bottom of the gravity of these processes should have put in place. Do not underestimate this aspect. In our country, a combined arrangement so powerful (economic crisis on the one hand and moral crisis on the other) we have never known it in recent history, and even less recent. Of course, there was a historical phase in which the economic crisis was very creeping, very strong and very pervasive. Think of the Italy that came out of the war, the black misery that passed through our country, the people who ran away and were forced to emigrate. However, within that historical phase so difficult and so complex and at that economic phase so creeping, at least you had a point of reference: a ruling class, a hope, a possibility. In short, yes, there was the economic crisis, but there was also the morality of politics, the constituent Fathers, the great stories that we have behind us, the twentieth century in all its weight, in this case yes truly extraordinary. On the other hand, there have been times when we have experienced opposing situations: great moral crisis, great democratic crisis, crisis of representation, political parties in a situation of great difficulty, but at least an economy that held or that, however, was not narrow in the tension with which it fights in recent years. We find ourselves, instead, in this historical phase, with a combined arrangement abnormal for the history of this country, in which these elements, the social crisis, the economic crisis and the moral crisis, are exactly the theme around which the element of crisis of representative democracy is determined. It is not that the crisis element of representative democracy is born from evening to morning. It is born within a historical path, which precisely begins in the past years, finds in the historical phase that we are experiencing today a maximum moment of crisis and translates into a very banal and very simple result to observe, which any observer can focus with his magnifying glass. Our country had historically seen an extraordinary participation in the forms of democratic life; think of the turnout in the political elections, as it has been determined over the last fifty years. Our country, from this point of view, has also been a beacon and a model for other European democracies. Instead, think about how today these elements have a profound impact on the fact that an increasingly significant piece of public opinion considers politics, when it's good for us, as something else, separate, different, that doesn't interest him and, when it's bad, even as something that sucks him (if I can use this expression a little'strong). Is this the state of our country today or not? Is it precisely in which the forms of democracy and democratic participation have been consumed or not? Is that also where the organized form of political parties has experienced an element of crisis that is likely to be irreversible or not? In this area of difficulty lies the decisive political error of this affair, which makes me say a very strong no, today in this House and tomorrow in the constitutional referendum that will take place. The basic question is: what tools could be imagined in the face of a crisis of this magnitude? There were two roads. Unfortunately, the wrong path has been chosen, the one that will accentuate this element of crisis and detachment. One way was to say: we recognize that there is a gigantic problem, that concerns representation and representativeness and we work, in this country, to rebuild this element of connection blown up between people and institutions. How? Through forms of democratic participation, through elements of profound enlargement of democracy, even through elements of counter-trend with respect to the general political line chosen in recent years. The general political line chosen in recent years, however, was another, exactly the one that is retraced in this constitutional reform, not only without changing the verse, but instead interpreting to the end that verse built in the narration of the last twenty years. The line according to which the main cause of this element of difficulty was to be found in the crisis of representation, was therefore to identify, as a remedy to the crisis of representation, the increase of the effectiveness of governability. This thesis was at the heart of a whole political reflection and was also rejected by the sovereign people in some past constitutional referendums. Even the experience of these years should have warned of the danger of this thesis, that perhaps in the short term it may also have an element of strength, but that in the medium and long term it is destined to produce a further element of worsening of the crisis already today very deep democracy in our country. So, I believe that at this basic question we have chosen to put the wrong political path into practice. When we said, turning in vain in these months, that another type of approach would be needed, we wanted to focus on another point that I would like to emphasize in this debate: method and merit, never as in a debate like this marked by the context with which we measure ourselves and have behind us, inevitably become an inescapable interweaving. I believe that that constituent spirit, which we have tried so many times to recall in this House, should have suggested another way forward. I have not yet understood, in the face of a crisis as serious as the one that is going through Italian democracy today, why one cannot think of choosing the main road, which is always and only one: to imagine a constituent Assembly, perhaps elected with a proportional law perfectly representative of the social and electoral bodies of the country, without prizes of majority that imbalance the real representativeness, that can put in place a cohesive and shared reasoning of how to change and how to improve the Italian Constitution. I, who are one of those who strongly defends the Italian Constitution, see that there are points on which elements of improvement should be introduced, also on the subject of bicameralism, which, however, would have deserved this type of approach and not the idea that the problem could be solved with the way taken. I believe that the Government's choices will not lead to the advance of the deep crisis that we are going through today, but rather will become a further moment of worsening of the crisis. I think, for these reasons, that another spirit would have been needed to do exactly the opposite of what has been done: the opposite of moving forward by majority vote, the opposite of what I have defined a thousand times in this House - and some of my colleagues have done so in the course of this debate - demagogy from above, which is imagined as an answer to populism, demagogy from below, but which, of course, was nothing more than by definition, is even more dangerous and, in any case, once again, in the long run, does not solve the problem, on the contrary, accentuates it. It would have taken the opposite of this plebiscytic idea for which the sovereign people are called to speak in a constitutional referendum, but instead of asking them what they think about constitutional reform, there is a plebiscite mechanism whereby "you're either with me or against me" by twisting the debate over how it would have been useful. Then there is the criticism of merit. I hope that in the election campaign - I am sure we will - when the reasons for yes and no can be no longer simply a matter that is being discussed in this House, but in the country, we will have time to put them in line one by one. I think it is a mistake - as I said just now - to emphasize the view that the crisis is resolved by increasing the powers of the Commission to the detriment of Parliament's powers. This is a dramatic mistake that you will regret when the political situation that will result will become an element of difficulty. It is clear that constitutional law and electoral law - I say nothing because it has been remembered a thousand times in the course of the debate - cannot be read as if they were two distant things. On the other hand, they are so intertwined that they represent here too some elements of torsion and even gigantic themes such as peace and war - a subject on which a very respectful methodological approach should be taken - risk being, in a combined arrangement such as this, discussion of a national direction of the only party that wins the elections. I'm worried about a situation like this. I also think - and we have said so many times - that the electoral mechanism imagined, apart from some proposals for amendment, remains, however, essentially, that of the second level. Madam Minister, I have gone astray to point out, in this House, that it has been very serious that Parliament has never taken stock of those second-level elections, which already exist in our country. Perhaps many observers are unaware that there are already some bodies in Italy, voting with second-level elections. These are the so-called metropolitan cities, which have replaced the Provinces. In all the cases in which we faced that type of second-level election, in the few months in which they took place, they represented the worst, the most vulgar and the most dramatic voting market, if we want to call it that, or very low-level trading. This was, at least in the Italian representation, the election of second level and, perhaps, before imagining that even one of the two Chambers of the Republic was elected with this type of mechanism, a review had to be made of how the second level elections worked. I therefore believe that this has been greatly missed. I also wonder whether it is ever possible that, in the face of such a creeping crisis of democratic representation, and in the face of the fact that, precisely, entire and increasing segments of the population look at politics as an increasingly distant thing, the theme has not been to work deeply on the instruments of direct democracy, strengthening them, actually making them instruments that could make a decisive contribution. However, this has not been done either. I therefore believe that, for all these reasons, the issue is not whether or not there is an authoritarian turning point or something like that. The issue is that this reform does not solve the crisis of Italian democracy, but unfortunately it will dramatically accentuate the elements of crisis, as we have known them. Of course, it is a reform that serves to say that something is being done, but I do not believe, honestly, that this is simply enough to put a hand in the Republican Constitution. Perhaps they would have served more modesty and humility than a Constitutional Charter that has its own history and dignity, so wide and extraordinary, that, perhaps, those who have tried to defend it, in these years, should have been heard more, rather than simply being labeled as the enemy of change. This House has been deaf to these elements, which have been on the table for many months now. She was deaf in the face of oppositions, although very different: we are faced with this real anomaly, so the reform will not be shared by oppositions who think in the opposite way about many things. These oppositions, which think in the opposite way about so many things, believe instead that, on the fundamental rules - on the rules: not on political lines and on political cultures - would have served instead that deepening, that synthesis and that progress, which would perhaps have allowed the country to try to change direction, compared to the deep crisis in which we ended in these years. However, I believe that this will not be the case, and I believe that, precisely, if this reform were to be adopted, if the referendum were to confirm it and if it were to be accompanied, as it will be, by the electoral law currently in force, this would be a dangerous, wrong and serious setback for Italian democracy. But I also think that, fortunately, the wisdom of the constituent Fathers, when they wrote the Constitutional Charter, many years ago, however, gave the sovereign people the opportunity to say the final word. In spite of everything, I believe very much in the seriousness, capacity and vision of the sovereign people and I think that, again, it will be able to do a better job than its representatives. ParlaMint-IT_2016-01-20-LEG17-Senato-sed-563.ana.u85	She is registered to speak Senator Chiavaroli. You have the right to do so.
ParlaMint-IT_2016-01-20-LEG17-Senato-sed-563.ana.u86	Mr President, Mrs Ministro, ladies and gentlemen, the draft Constitutional Reform Act that we are about to vote on in this House has entrusted us with a costly and very responsible task. Today we are changing the structure of our fundamental Charter, the set of rules that it has guaranteed for over sixty years - certainly now more effectively now less - a full democratic function. Today we are changing our country and with it our future. How long has it been since we first began to support the need to change the Constitution? For more than thirty years, Italy has been waiting for a reform of its institutional structure, due to the obvious limitations it has shown in guaranteeing the country a timely and thoughtful decision-making capacity. In the past, other illustrious colleagues tried, without ever succeeding in that attempt to prevail over the fear of change. They were accused, then and now, of authoritarianism by those same ones who now as then organize and mobilize the committees of the no, which however has always been a no regardless. Let me say that it is regrettable to see today stand on the same rejection positions regardless of the right-wing parties, for reasons that appear to be more linked to political contingency than to their own history and perspective. However, ladies and gentlemen, it is no longer the time of cat paddling. We can't do this anymore. Let's have it. We owe it to our children, to whom we have already taken too much of their hopes and their future. It is not the exasperated decision-making of a coalition government that supports it, but it is the times and ways of politics. There is a need to reform an institutional system that dates back to an outdated historical era and has shown all its limitations. It is the new international structures, the developments of the financial markets, the emergency emigration, the problems of security, the profound social crisis before economic and political that we are still wounded to impose on us a modernization of our institutional system, which can no longer be postponed. We must not, we cannot leave to future generations a country unable to face political and economic unpredictability with the right times and ways. At the outbreak of the sovereign debt crisis and the real economy, Italy proved to be vulnerable, more than the other countries of the European Union, because of an institutional system that was not able to react quickly and that did not seem able to express a stable political direction. We all in this House cannot have forgotten the stalemate that has been created since the 2013 general elections. This legislature was born with difficulty, without a majority, without the possibility of forming a new Government, nor of being able to elect a new President of the Republic. It was the generous contribution of President Napolitano and the representatives of the two most representative coalitions that made it possible to put an end to an institutional stalemate that would bring the country to its knees, which would make it even more vulnerable to the eyes of the European allies, exposed to the worst financial speculations. This reform writes the conditions so that what happened, that is, the birth of a government of broad understanding, no longer takes place for necessity and imposition. Of course, what we are about to approve is not the perfect reform. This is not the same as it was in 1948. Just read the acts and statements of those months that led to the promulgation of the Charter to understand that it was the result of a compromise that many then considered downward, but necessary if we wanted to avoid the risk of a new authoritarian drift. That is not the perfect reform either. A little by definition - as well as for the 1948 Charter, which has long been considered the best Constitution in the world, we will discover only by living the limits - and much for the internal compromises of the majority that approved it. For example, a limit is the failure to amend the articles of the Constitution relating directly to the form of government, so there will be no constitutional arrangements suitable to protect the stability requirements of the government, as requested by the famous Perassi agenda, approved by the constituent assembly. Another limit is the failure to provide for a closing rule for the election of the President of the Republic. The quorum of the three fifths could lead to a dangerous impasse, while it would have been preferable to extend the electoral college of the President of the Republic, providing for a quorum equal to the absolute majority. But this reform will not be the death of Parliament, as - alas! - I have heard in this House. It will not be the end of democracy, but it will not be yet another missed opportunity. On the contrary, this reform is born to strengthen the form of parliamentary government, enhancing the seat of representation in a more narrow and efficient dialectic with the majority of government chosen by voters. Thanks to this reform we will no longer be able to hide behind the lengths of the stalls between the two rooms, we will no longer be able to play the "download barrel" between the two branches of Parliament. The simplification of the legal process will be an opportunity to concentrate and make the debate in Parliament more effective on fundamental choices. And Parliament's work will be clear to the voters, who will therefore have greater powers to judge their work. What an end to democracy! The citizens, ladies and gentlemen, as we know, are losing confidence in the institution that we have the honour to represent for the "slow-cracy" of vetoes and countervetes of an old bicameralism equal. For this reason, I think it is extremely important to introduce the so-called preferential lane for the designs essential for the implementation of the government programme, by resolution on a certain date, within a maximum of 90 days. A sufficient time to discuss and improve the draft law, which is an essential prerogative of Parliament. Once, however, at the end of which it is necessary to decide. Only in this way will we be able to have governments that will avoid abusing the decree of urgency, the maximaments and the votes of confidence, the way in which any executive has used to govern. Those who believe that this reform will lead to an authoritarian drift are deeply mistaken. Suffice it to say that the Government, as has already been pointed out, will not have the powers that other European democracies have. The President-in-Office of the Council will not be able to revoke the ministers, either to propose an early appeal to the ballot box, or to veto the deliberations which impose burdens on public finances. But there is another aspect that I would like to stress here today. Finally, this reform aims to straighten out the crooked tree of that federalism in which the irresponsibility of regional and local administrators prevailed. The transformation of the Senate into the Senate of the autonomy: no longer, therefore a second Political Chamber, but a Chamber that will represent the territorial autonomy in order to overcome the errors posed by the modification of Title V realized in 2001. That reform, hasty we add today with proof of the facts, with a misconceived division of competences between the central state and the regions, has in fact fueled the constitutional dispute, multiplying the holders of veto powers ending to paralyze the system, with enormous damage to the economy of our country. The new Title V, on the other hand, gives centrality to the unitary State. However, the recentralisation of a number of subjects, improperly attributed to the competence shared by the 2001 revision, will be largely compensated by the participation of Regions and Municipalities in the implementation of national public policies and state laws, through the presence in the Senate of their representatives. I hope that future members of the Senate will understand the importance of this objective. I would like to remind everyone that before this last reading, the reform has been widely shared in Parliament, including by political opposition forces. To speak of an institutional rift of the majority seems to me to be somewhat out of place. This reform is primarily the result of the work of the Committee of Wise Men set up ad hoc by the Letta Government, of the work of the Constitutional Affairs Commissions that improved the text presented by the Government, thanks also to the contribution of colleagues from Forza Italia who voted favorably for the first reading both in the Senate and in the House. What we are about to achieve here today, ladies and gentlemen, is not the point of arrival, but it is only a fundamental stage of a much longer path of institutional, economic and social reforms, which have the objective of achieving the project of a better country than what has been entrusted to us. The citizens will have the last word in the referendum, but we are sure that they will be able to share with all of us that today we are preparing to vote for reform by building a more modern, more efficient, more competitive state. [[Applause from the AP Group (NCD-UDC). Congratulations.]]

What are the perplexities? I confess (but perhaps this is my lack) that I do not have a regionalist mindset, that I do not have an autonomousist sensibility. I am a lover of the state-form, which I have crossed in his great and troubled modern story, of the State as the holder of the monopoly of legitimate force according to the classic Weberian definition. And I think that today, when national sovereignty is being discussed and overtaken, it is precisely today, in this great transition, that we need not less State, but more State. It is a beautiful utopia for people to make a political Europe. This new Europe, in fact, could only be done by States if they made this political decision: the States, not the governments. I would have liked to have seen the constitutional reform discussed and elaborated within this context of speech. Never, as at this time here, in the Italian contingency, would we need to prepare an institutional place, a high chamber in the literal sense of the word, with the task, I would say with the imperative mandate to ensure and re-establish relations, in order to use the traditional terms, between the legal country and the real country, between institutions and citizens, between rulers and governed, in a word between the elite and the people. This report today is abruptly, violently interrupted. Maybe we don't realize it, and it's true that sometimes we say it, but to go right away to do and talk about something else. On the other hand, the problem must be dealt with in large numbers. The Constituents had imagined such a form of Senate when, in the first legislature, they sent men and women who had been subjected to years of fascist imprisonment and when they established the figure of senators for life, who gave and still give lust to this branch of Parliament. Well, I imagined a Senate that, freed from the trust relationship with the Government on duty, freed from ordinary legislation, composed of a small number of highly worthy personalities could concentrate its work on reconnecting that interrupted relationship. Today, the only institution that still holds the confidence of its citizens is the Presidency of the Republic, thanks also to the last excellent Presidents. But this institution performs this function in absolute solitude. He'd need an operating body. I address you, who are also able to grasp the nuances of the speech. I am not so naive as to consider this solution feasible in this form. That's how I say it, I put things like that, to exasperate the problem. Colleagues, all of us who sit in these seats are accused, and not because of a more or less good electoral law, more or less bad, but because of something more serious, because of a widespread popular judgment. We find it hard to say out of here that we are here, that we are doing this job, that we are doing this function. And I don't know about you colleagues, but I live this condition with great suffering. There is this black humor that circulates - as they say - in the belly of the country. It is not a criticism of politics - perhaps it was - it is a hatred of politics, it is contempt for those who practice it, it is a declaration of distrust of the same word "political." This situation must not be endured, nor must it be ride: it must be changed. We have before us the theoretical and practical problem of how and when contemporary democracies, current democratic systems, have become producers and breeding, in an enlarged form, of anti-political. I know for a fact that today's democratic forms certify a sort of dictatorship of opinion. We must distinguish between consensus and opinion. A political problem today is how to treat consensus, rightly, without remaining victims and subordinates to opinion. Anti-politics is today the enemy to be beaten for all of us who are here, majorities and minorities. It is said that this anti-political black humor is more than justified, given the performance of a certain political class, more or less recent. True, but the justification for one thing doesn't make that thing right. An example of these days: it is right to proceed with the decriminalization of crimes of illegality. It is also appropriate, as industry operators, judiciary and law enforcement support. But it can't be done, because people's perception of insecurity wouldn't change. The task of politics is to demonstrate, to make people understand, to convince that concentrating that perception on that point is wrong. Politics, of course, must listen, but it must also be able to speak, to speak to people and, by this way, to direct and direct. This is its natural function. It is precisely when it does not perform this function that it is not recognized and is sometimes rightly contested. I am convinced of one thing: if you do not get out of anti-politics, there is no chance of effective good government. Because the government is often forced to pursue these impulses and adapt its decisions to them. And beware, there is not even a chance of good opposition, because the opposition will even be led to anticipate the Government in listening to those instances that are nobly defined as basic. Then, political decision, which is a serious matter, cannot go to the poll, which is not a serious thing; so they produce only wrong laws and more confusion and disorientation. I want to say one thing to the citizens 5 Stars, and do not take it as a lesson that the professor does, because I say it with sympathy. By growing consensus around you, you are rightly proposing yourself as the next force of government. Beware, to be a force of government, you must learn the art of governing, which is the most difficult, complex and complicated political art. Keep in mind that administering and governing are two different things: one is far below, the other much above. Government colleagues, I address you directly. I always record a thrill of reaction when I hear the formula pronounced "the mayor of Italy." No, Italy, like any great country, does not need a mayor, perhaps not enough under these conditions not even a traditional President of the Council: it needs a Head of Government who dresses up the figure of the man of state. I think our young President is able to do this for the good of the country. Mr President, I should like to conclude by making a brief statement, each of which would require much more arguments, not least because I am not in the tradition of the part of the House that I am talking about. The crisis of politics is: it is a crisis of its modern foundations, so it is not something new. For me, from what I understand, and of course I can make a mistake, the crisis in politics today, not in general, in this contingency, is less crisis of representation and more crisis of authority. It has happened to me on other occasions to observe, always with the irony that we must keep in the field, but also with truth, that it represents even too much, it represents everything, and all passively, without hierarchicalizing the questions, according to necessity and urgency. And I sometimes see the manifestation of primary needs and the needs of secondary rights being put on the same footing. That's not gonna work. From here, too, comes the fall of political authority. Auctoritas is recognized power, and only thus becomes true, effective, efficient potestas, that is, power that responsibly decides. And today this recognition is not there, it must be rebuilt and it must be regained. Moreover, the problem of politics, here and now, is less a problem of legality and more a problem of legitimacy. Large categories of these, legality and legitimacy, which modern politics has long thought of and which today need to be rethought and re-proposed. Politics must be re-legitized and re-legitized by the political class in the sense and in the name that our elites gave it: Pareto, Moscow, Michels. I would say that this process concerns more generally the ruling classes. The two traditional places of political class selection, Parliament and parties, have been dangerously emptied and sterilized. Those who have had responsibility for this have a great and negative responsibility, because they are not concerned about making new ones. And then please -- this is my personal thing I want to say -- don't tell me that this is what they do or could do, once they've been regulated, the primary. If for once randomly select the best, nine times punctually select the worst. The big problem is to reconstruct professionally capable, ethically irreproachable, politically responsible ruling classes; the only way, among other things, to reasonably delimit the present excessive personalization of politics. And the restart is - mind you - from above. As you can see, that reason of the Upper House was only, in the form of suggestion and small utopia, the way to introduce some basic themes into the debate on this essential and fundamental passage of reform of our Constitution. [[Applause from the PD and AP groups (NCD-UDC).]]

[[Applause from the PD and AP groups (NCD-UDC).]] I am not only here for a list of thanks; I am here to pay tribute, gentlemen of the Senate, to you, a not formal but sincere and profound tribute. I think the story will take care of this day. Italian political history will take care of this day. [[Ironic applause from the M5S Group]] The story will be kind to you, kind senators and dear senators. As a great British statesman would have said, you chose to write the story and not read it. The gesture you have made twice by agreeing with an absolute majority vote to overcome the Senate has no equal, not in Italian history, but in the history of the European institutions. The gesture you have made against self-referentiality by blocking that bias belt against politics reaffirms, Senator Tronti, the centrality of the public thing as absolute value, regardless of the discussions of one side and the other. We can agree or not with the reform. As you know, we do not have the same opinions, but the country owes you, kind senators and dear senators, an institutional gratitude to which I want to add my personal. Two years ago I took the floor in this House to present the government that the then President of the Republic had instructed me to form. It was a very slender government, the second most slender in the history of the 63 Republican governments in numerical terms and the one with the highest presence of women and with the lowest age level in republican history. I myself, when I spoke here in the Senate, was not old enough to sit among you. The government had such a demanding goal that it played for some of you unrealistic: to put in place and carry out all the reforms that, for better or for worse, had been promised to Italian citizens and on which this country had committed itself at European level. It was a program considered ambitious by friends, daring by observers and impossible by opponents. It was February 2014, not even 24 months ago. The country's GDP had closed the year to -1,9 percent, partaking of the sad performance of the previous year (-2,3). Unemployment was over 13 percent and youth unemployment exceeded 46 percent according to ISTAT data. The confidence indices of consumers and businesses were at the very least historic, and confidence in politics and institutions was also largely negative. In Europe, references to Italy were made in common with Greece at all times, and the key words of the continental debate were only privatisation and austerity. We accepted a challenge at the most difficult time and, when I spoke in this House before you, I began with a provocation. Some of you, ladies and gentlemen, including my party, have still not forgiven me, and I do not think it was for the hands in your pocket or for the speech in your arms. You did not forgive me because the provocation was very strong: I want to be the last President of the Council who asks for confidence in this House. I remember the looks and I remember the smiles, but if this Assembly, in a few hours will confirm the vote already expressed in August 2014 and October 2015, that forecast, which seemed bold, will become a reality. And it is thanks to your commitment, in these two years, if the situation of the country is different from February 2014. Gross domestic product (GDP) has risen from -1.9% to +0.8%, unemployment has fallen just above 11%, youth has steadily fallen below 40%, confidence indices are at their peak over the last 15 years and consumption is growing again. Italy's not good. Italy is better; and Italy is better because politics has shown that, believing us, reforms can be carried out. Then we can divide in merit, but after years of drunkenness of acronyms, like TINA (there is no alternative: there are no alternatives), of considerations regarding the uselessness of politics, of subalternity of politics with respect to economic and financial phenomena, after years in which the quainquism has gone on stage even in the details, politics has taken its place in this country, with an organic design, carried forward by the Parliament of the Republic, in its two branches - the Senate of the Republic and the Chamber of Deputies - whose borders are finally visible today. We have therefore begun to remodel the institutional, economic and administrative systems. In the time of what some thinkers call the "vetocracy," that is, the power given to those who have the right to veto, we have made it easier to decide. In doing so, it is as if we had returned to Italy a right, the right to the future, which was denied by those who had a shrill idea of the future of this country. Therefore, we can think of it in every way, we can have the most disparate opinions, we can believe and divide ourselves on everything, but we cannot deny that, in this parliamentary term, this Parliament - which I am not a member of - has, over such a short period of time, put hand to the reform of an electoral law, that a decision of the Constitutional Court had judged illegitimate, getting to the point of putting the political situation in check and stall, and has created, within a few months, a reform of the work, on whose evaluation will be the history, or perhaps the chronicle, to say what we have done. There is no doubt, however, that the ISTAT and INPS data of the last few days show, in an unmistakable way, that finally the curve has returned to growth: +510,000 permanent employment contracts, which means, for a boy, for a young man, not a sheet of paper, but the possibility of a right to the future, to a home, to a child and to a perspective [[Applause from the PD, AP (NCD-UDC) and Aut (SVP, UV, PATT, UPT) and PSI-MAIE groups)]] Then there is the reform of the public administration (which will see the first legislative decrees come out of the Council of Ministers this evening) the reduction of taxes considered unjustly or rightly unbearable [[Comments of Senator Martelli]] , such as the first house tax, but also the work component of the IRAP or those related to agriculture. I could continue, from the topic that I feel most dear, that of international cooperation, to issues related to civil justice. I do not go into the merits of the individual reforms, I say that with today an extraordinary path is closed, which has marked the path of the last two years, with a metronome of emergency, which has seen Italy do, in a very short time, things that, for years had stood still in the swamp of the stalemate: the clearer Title V, never again the double trust, the simplified legislative procedure, the abolition from the Constitution of useless bodies (assumed, of course). There is, however, a key point in this debate, and I would like to mention it here in the Senate, not because it does not apply to the House of Deputies, but because here there is the political value of what happened: overcoming a prejudice that eclipsed confidence in public affairs and politics, that is, the idea that legislators could not intervene in their own branch of Parliament. This is the key point, which is changing the perception of politics by citizens. How many dreamed of this moment? Generations of parliamentarians have imagined that we could overcome the bicameralism of equality. The constituent assembly, for those who had the fortune, even at the university, to be able to read those extraordinary papers, had discussed for a long time on the model of bicameralism and we must recognize that equal (or perfect) bicameralism was considered the compromise of then, was not the first choice of any of the political forces in the field. The debate of the following seventy years [[Comments from the M5S Group]] has seen the Commission Bozzi, the Commission De Mita, the Commission Iotti, the Commission D'Alema, the work of the 2000s constantly coming back to this point: overcoming the bicameralism equal. This is not, however, the greatest value of today; it is not in this great phase passage, as we would have said. The greatest value is that this is the day that an entire generation of parliamentarians called the day that never had to come; this is the day that no one believed we would arrive and we arrived because you had courage and confidence. I would like to make just one point on the subject of the reform, and I would like to say it to the opposition, with the respect that is due to those who will not vote and to those who have expressed - I must say very clearly - negative assessments of the text or, as has happened, the way in which this reform is presented. You can agree or not; you can - I say it with great respect to Senator Paolo Romani - have changed your mind from the initial path, which was deliberately inclusive and broad; you may have changed your mind with all the sweet technical considerations of this world, also because I do not forget that this is a legislature in which some Group has defined fascist a bill approved in a branch of Parliament by the same Group, but in the component of the other House. I am therefore aware that there have been discussions within the parties at this time, which I respect. However, there is one point that I want to say clearly and that paradoxically can even be regarded as the weak point even by someone who will vote in favour of this reform: we do not touch the system of weights and counterweights provided for in the Constitutional Charter. [[Comments from the M5S Group. Applause from the PD Group. Ironic applause from the M5S Group. Senator Cioffi's comments.]] This must remain clear. I say this because here, even among those who vote in favour of reform, there are those who argue - and I respect it - that not having chosen presidentialism or semi-presidency, not making this choice, is a mistake. This bill of constitutional reform, which modifies bicameralism, which intervenes on the powers of the Regions, which cancels forever the CNEL and the Provinces from the constitutional vocabulary [[Comments from the M5S Group),]] does not affect the role of the Presidency of the Republic and counterweight bodies, as defined by the Constitutional Council of 1946. If there is this - and there is - I believe that intellectual honesty is needed to note that this reform, quite simply, seeks to make a parliamentary system less plastered, that over the last seventy years has produced 63 governments and that it presents a quality of the lawmakers of the highest level, but that it has procedures so cumbersome that they are unanimously considered to change. The legislature that started didn't have a future. However, together with the reform of the electoral system, which was necessary, it guarantees at least governance, which is an indispensable condition for things to finally be done in the country. Today this system, unlike the previous one, finally sees the end of the perfect bicameralism. Therefore this reform, precisely with the end of the perfect bicameralism and along with the reform of the electoral law (which, I remind everyone, was necessary), finally clarifies who wins. We will know the outcome of the votes, that only one will win, and that one will have the right to rule. For Italian citizens, priority must be given, in the words and language of politics, to issues related to work, to our businesses, to workers, to the family, to agriculture, to trade. These must be the issues that must become a priority in political action. We then say enough about immobilism, enough about the swamp that paralyzes any change. For us, what is important is that the reform process in the country continues. Between this reform - which I repeat, is not the best text possible - and no reform is however preferable to have a change, which is what the Italians rightly claim. We will listen carefully to the statements that will take place in this House, because we are considering the path of reform and we want to see how the path of change will be. But one thing is certain: we say enough to the swamp, enough to the games of obstruction, always and anyway, enough to the politics of the much worse so much the better. We are considering, and certainly ours will not be against, because we think that reforms must go ahead and that they must be carried out. Immobilism and swamp leave them to others.
