ParlaMint-IT_2016-01-20-LEG17-Senato-sed-563.ana.u118	BONFRISCO (CoR). And I work hard to respect this House, Mr President. To the President-in-Office of the Council, I would like to say that if the President-in-Office of the Council calls for a response to the general debate on constitutional reform, we call it a dangerous self-exaltation, which makes us even more appreciate the work of Mr Boschi, who in this House has tried, every day of the long debate on reforms, to share and confront the whole House on the substance of the text, a reform text to which I repeat right from the start, and I start from the end, our conviction is not. Ours is not convinced, Mr President, certainly not because my Group considers the Constitution to be an untouchable totem; on the contrary, we Conservatives and Reformists believe that we can and must change it in order to make it consistent with the changed needs of our country. It is that I regret, Mr President, to see how this important reform opportunity has been lost, despite the desperate need of the country for a real modern restructuring of its institutional system. Gentlemen of the majority, you started at the beginning of the debate, rightly I say, from the need for reforms for the modernization of the country. What did you do, though? You have adopted the solution of exchanging a real legislative assembly with a wheelchair, whose composition rather than participation seems to respond only to the logic of the consolation prizes for local politicians. You could have abolished this Senate, as we proposed. Why did you refuse? Thanks to your lack of reforms, thanks also to the nos you have said to the proposals for modification of my Group, in Italy you will be able to continue without limits to pursue expenses with revenues, to abuse the incomes and assets of citizens, without a rag of guarantee that puts embankments both to the unlimited fame of the money of the State and to its waste. You have also narrowed the areas of regional autonomy, rather than challenging the regions to a qualitative leap in the management of taxes and charges equivalent to taxes. You, on the other hand, are centralized and de-responsible, rather than moving in the opposite direction. Mr President, I say this to you above all, for me a reform can only be worthy of this name in one way, that is, with full respect for the procedures and procedures that the Constitutional Charter itself imposes - it would be better to say, it would have imposed - to guarantee the fundamental nature of its provisions which represent the state's core architecture. This reform - and I say this with sincere regret - is not worthy of this name. It is not because a real expropriation of sovereignty has taken place, a real expropriation carried out by the Government to the detriment of the entire Parliament and therefore of the citizens. It is not because a constitutional reform cannot be called Boschi-Renzi (or Renzi-Boschi, as we have learned today) bringing back to the government an activity that the Constitution reserves for initiative and parliamentary action. It is a mistake already made in the past: the Berlusconi Government did it and we all know how it ended. It is not because the Senate, which will have to be elected with a bizarre system to be determined by ordinary law, has been configured as a sort of Chamber of Autonomies, which would have had a sense if sewn into a model of federal state (such as Italy is not, and unfortunately shows that it does not want to be), also in the light of the reform of Title V, that operated by you, also on this occasion of marked return to a centralizing mold. It is not, because the choice to have the senators-advisors elected by the regional councils, as written by you, will generate many uncertainties, since the councilors seem subject to a sort of double investiture (popular and council), while instead the election of the mayors will remain exclusive business of the regional councils and their discretion. Not to mention the option on how to link the popular vote to the council election, which was entirely postponed to a bicameral electoral law. Mrs. Boschi, therefore, pending the intervention of the ordinary legislator (who knows when it will happen), the Regional Councils will elect the members of the new Senate. The transitional provision indicates how to proceed with the election with a proportional method, but without any provision on the first election, the real one, the one that should be popular, whose discipline is entirely postponed to a very uncertain and future electoral law. If we then analyze the large number of times in which the deadlines set by the constitutional norms have been disregarded by the ordinary legislator (think of the recent case of federalism), there is a very high risk that even this form of indirect election, even that little election that exists, will remain dead letter. This is only a case, although emblematic, of the rosary of laws that will have to be approved in order to implement decisive aspects of constitutional reform. Like that on the modalities and also on the incisiveness of the controls of the Senate on government appointments, or that in the matter of equal opportunities, dear colleagues of the PD, for women in political positions, who risk concretely to remain yet another commercial, without the intervention of the ordinary legislator (who knows when it will come). Even a mediocre jurist - and I am not even that - would be able to understand that on fundamental issues reform does not decide, but merely postpones any decision to laws that perhaps, at the heart of future majorities, will be made in the future. We are actually about to vote not on a constitutional change, but on a constitutional law that delegates. I think this is the first time this has happened in this Parliament and it is not a good record, Mr President-in-Office of the Council. That is why this is a reform that does not reform and that, thanks also to the new electoral law, will consecrate the government's overpower over Parliament, a Parliament reduced to a rag. There is no longer the Senate, whose role could be compensated by an increase in the powers of the Head of State (our guarantor by name), whose powers, on the contrary, have also been reduced to a light. Let us bear in mind, ladies and gentlemen, that it will no longer be the President of the Republic who will hand over the keys of the Government to the President of the Council, but the majority prize of an electoral law (which you voted on) that distorts democracy, as we have always denounced. But not only that. The President of the Republic has even been deprived of the power to dissolve the Senate and, implicitly, also the Chamber, which will be in the hands of the leader of the majority party that will be crowned thanks to the Italianum, which will decree its life or death. In fact, we are discussing here a change that changes the verse - as Prime Minister Renzi likes to say - but in the opposite direction: against popular sovereignty, which is further compressed and cancelled. That's the point. An open conflict is created with the so-called supreme principles of the order, among which, precisely, the one for which - ahivoj! - sovereignty belongs to the people. Today we are called to ratify the snatch to the citizens of two important electoral ballots: the one for the Senate, but also the referendum. In fact, instead of eliminating the quorum for the validity of the referendums, you have raised the threshold of signatures necessary to present it. Not to mention the laws of popular initiative, whose necessary signatures have even fivefold. Put together then the fact that - as I have already said - the senators will no longer be elected by the citizens and the circle of your complicated relationship with the popular vote closes. You have been bickering with universal suffrage since 1948. At the time, the call to the polls, for the first time, of Italian women made the victory fade already in the pocket of the People's Front. It happened again in 1994, even when you had a joyous war machine, but the Italian people once again said no to you. It is precisely to that people that Renzi will address in the long electoral campaign on the referendum, which will not concern the merit or, indeed, the demerit of the reforms, but his person, in his role as President of the Council, who is also secretary of the party. All we need is for you to take on the position of spiritual leader and that's how I turn you into a democracy - alas! - imperfect as any, in a democracy that only Orwell could have imagined. But since we're in the time of "Matrix" and not those of Orwell; is that why perhaps it is necessary for some party friends to take care of cybersecurity? I can already hear President Renzi say, on unified networks, that he has suppressed the Senate. I wish he had! We will also hear him say that the laws will get faster. He will then say that he has reduced the costs of politics and that he has increased the guarantees of governability. I can already hear the echo of these refrains. ParlaMint-IT_2016-01-20-LEG17-Senato-sed-563.ana.u129	Mr Mazzoni (AL-A). Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, from 1982 to the present day, from the 8th to the 17th, there has been no legislature in republican history in which the node of constitutional reforms has not been addressed, and all the draft laws presented have raised the problem of achieving a thorough revision of the second part of the Constitution. If all these attempts have failed, there is a reason. Italy - and its political class is its mirror - has remained divided and perched behind the old ideological barriers: first there were the communists and the anti-communists, the Christian Democrats and the anti-Hemochristians, the Craxians and the anti-Craxians; then, in the second Republic, the Berlusconians and the anti-Berlusconians, in a crescendo of grudges and mutual delegitimation that penalized the country, its development and above all the quality of its democracy. The debate of these days, unfortunately, shows that nothing or almost has changed, because today politics continues to divide itself prejudiceally, and this time between Renziani and anti-Rentians, and in a Pavlovian way repeats the same patterns customary, the same accusations artifacts, the same insults regardless, of when the Government was Berlusconi, of which the left said that it had authoritarian impulses, that with it Italy counted nothing in Europe and that it was to be cast out in the supreme interest of safeguarding constitutional values. Yesterday the aspiring tyrant was Silvio Berlusconi, today Matteo Renzi became him, and, in oddly reversed parts, the center-right - the same as Monti and Letta accused of being succubs of the European Union - droves against the current Premier, because he dares raise his voice against the bureaucrats of Brussels. It is, in short, a sadly repetitive scenario, a alternating political fascism that led the left to sink the constitutional reform of the center-right in the 2006 referendum, and now causes the center-right to hurricane itself against this reform, which first shared and helped to write and then inexplicably repudiated. It is a dawn of fascism that we do not want proudly to enroll in. When Parliament launches this reform, we will finally confront each other in the country, and it will then be interesting to see the spectacle of the fans of the most beautiful Constitution in the world and of the former reformists of the center-right, who for twenty years have faced each other on opposite sides, go hand in hand to support in front of the citizens that in Italy we are at the front of the institutional coup. And he will be very sorry to see the head of this new violet people, of this counter-reformist roundabout, precisely the leader of Forza Italia, the liberal who modernised politics and called for years to strengthen the powers of the Premier, but who now denounces the authoritarian drift, thus finding himself in singular harmony with his pervicacious opponent, Professor Zagrebelsky, founder of the committee of the No, for which with the bill Boschi we assist the arming of power, its concentration in the hands of the Executive to the detriment of Parliament and citizens. The truth is another: after almost seventy years, Parliament is finally implementing the Perassi agenda, which committed the constituent Assembly to finding constitutional arrangements to protect the stability requirements of government action and to avoid the degeneration of parliamentarism. Well, electoral reform and constitutional reform have precisely the ambition to deliver to the country not an autarchic regime, but a more stable government and parliamentarism without degeneration, because based on the clear distinction of roles between a House holder of the relationship of trust with the Executive and a Senate of the self-governments, thus overcoming that bicameralism equal that has become the ball at the foot of our democracy. This is exactly what has been happening for some time in so many other Western democracies that have electoral laws with implicit or explicit ultra-majoritarian awards and a monocameral system, but where no one has ever allowed himself to overshadow authoritarian risks. This is a historic step towards a more mature democracy. Having a stronger Executive does not mean emptying parliamentary prerogatives. It only means guaranteeing political stability and putting behind us the Assemblyism that has become a system that has produced so many weak governments and too many Premier travicelli. Here I open a parenthesis to launch a warning to the sailors: the Italicum revised and updated because of the compromises to the bottom, that Renzi has had to do with his party and his coalition of government, that it previews a prize of majority of only 24 MPs, risks to return the Executives that will come, in spite of the constitutional reform, to the political blackmails of organized minorities - and in the PD, orphan of the democratic centralism, are particularly aggressive - able to assert at all times their power of veto. In short, it would be necessary to revise the new electoral law, but for the reason opposed to the one supported by those who shout at the coup and man only in command. Having said that, I wonder, however, how it is possible that those who shared the spirit and substance of the devolution in 2005 can today cut off this reform with such virulence, which will also be the reform of the devil, but ask us why then, yesterday as today, it was expected that the Senate would be representative of the interests of the territory. Yesterday, as today, it was proposed to reduce the number of Members (770 and 730); yesterday, as today, it was intended to abolish bicameralism. In addition, both reforms bring to the head of the state some strategic issues that the revision of Title V had delivered to the Regions, giving rise to a perennial institutional litigation. [[Brusio. Call the president back.]] Is the time available to me over, Mr President? PRESIDENT. - The debate is closed. No. I was trying to draw the attention of those who lovelyly do their own business and disturb them. Please, Senator Mazzoni. Mr Mazzoni (AL-A). Thank you, Mr President. Finally, both reforms strengthen state substitution power as a guarantee of national unity. Of course, this is not a perfect reform, starting from the mess about the direct eligibility of the new Senate and the lack of the Premier's power to dissolve the Chambers, but rather an imperfect reform than no reform. And to those who dispute that there will be an imbalance in the relationship of force between the House and the Government and between the Government and the Head of State it suffices to remember that there are instead many counterweights previewed: to elect the President of the Republic - for example - the only absolute majority will no longer be sufficient, but that of the three fifths, and the Senate will elect autonomously two constitutional judges. Not only that: the cases of recourse to decree-laws are limited and the statute of opposition is finally provided. And these - I would stress this - are not only the reforms of the Prime Minister or the Government, but also and above all the reforms of Parliament and the political forces that have helped to rewrite them, because the original text that came out of the Council of Ministers has been greatly improved. Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, today this House will be writing a decisive page in the political history of our country. After a decade of debate, we will finally come to the modernisation of the institutional system. We believe that we have taken a responsible position, in the awareness that it is not in the interest of the country to set aside good for the best and that the policy of continuous revival means only the continuation of the war to reforms by other means. We do not intend, therefore, to mix with the Brancariform army of the girotondini of all time and those climbed on the train of conservative maximumism. I therefore confirm the vote in favour of this draft Constitutional Reform Act by the Liberal Popular-Autonomous Group. In April, the path began on 18 January 2014, the day when the leaders of the center-left and centre-right pressed the so-called Nazarene pact. Two years have passed since that meeting and next October, a thousand days later, there will be a confirmation referendum. We had hoped that this pact, with Berlusconi crossing the threshold of the Nazarene, would definitively close twenty years of sterile opposition and paralysing ideological confrontation. That was not the case, and it was a mistake that the Italian people will first have to judge. But we have the belief that we have chosen the right part. [[Applause from the AL-A Group. Congratulations.]]
ParlaMint-IT_2016-01-20-LEG17-Senato-sed-563.ana.u130	I ask you to speak for an explanation of vote.
ParlaMint-IT_2016-01-20-LEG17-Senato-sed-563.ana.u131	You have the right to do so.
ParlaMint-IT_2016-01-20-LEG17-Senato-sed-563.ana.u132	ZELLER (Aut (SVP, UV, PATT, UPT)-PSI-MAIE). Mr President, at the beginning of this parliamentary term no one would have said that Parliament and, in the first place, this Senate would have been able to approve the constitutional reforms that have been expected for decades, especially when we consider the many past attempts at reform that have failed despite far greater majorities. I myself took part in the work of the bicameral Commission chaired by Massimo D'Alema in the 1990s and in the so-called Calderoli reform ten years ago. Both, after a start that seemed promising, did not reach the desired goal. The experiences I have mentioned serve to show that the objective of good reform is not enough, but that we must also take into account the political feasibility of the reform itself, which inevitably leads to mediations compared to what the proposers initially wanted. We are in the presence of an ambitious draft reform, which will not be the best and which certainly has some shortcomings, but which has so far found the consent of this Parliament. The Senate, in particular, has shown great responsibility: rarely in republican history does a parliamentary institution decide to strip itself of much of its powers. While the House has not undergone significant changes, the Senate has reformed itself, losing much of its powers and demonstrating the greatest possible openness to the revision and change of its role, composition, functions and competences. The overcoming of the bicameralism of equality, the significant reduction in the number of parliamentarians and the transformation of the Senate into a Chamber of representation of territorial autonomy are - in our opinion - objectives achieved. I do not deny that we, as the Group for the Self-Government, would have hoped for a strengthening of the regionalist system for the reform of Title V, version 2001, while we are now facing a return to the centralization of powers and a weakening of the ordinary regions. It is also true that we are facing a reform promoted by the Government, in particular by the President-in-Office of the Council and Minister Boschi, which therefore has its centre of gravity in favour of the Executive and the leader of the winning party by the political elections. It is also true, however, that, in the various passages (today we are in the fifth passage in the parliamentary classrooms), there has been a real parliamentary confrontation, sometimes even harsh, and we will certainly be able, beyond the vote, to continue to reason on the necessary institutional balances. But we do not share the position of those who present this reform as completely free of counterweights to the reinforced role of the Government. And it is not a reform that, together with the new electoral law, introduces into our institutional and parliamentary system a deficit of democracy or establishes the end, even, of the Republic born with the Constitutional Charter. The democratic system sanctioned by the parliamentary mechanism remains, but it becomes simpler and leaner, eliminating that anomaly that was the perfect bicameralism, where two Chambers did exactly the same things and were both holders of the relationship of trust with the Government. And the renewed constitutional organization introduces the more rational system of the legislative process, leaving to the Senate only a few and less incisive forms of control and intervention, except for constitutional laws and few other laws. It is a necessary and indivisible reform, as President Emeritus Giorgio Napolitano has rightly said, which has played a decisive role in supporting the constitutional reform process. And we take advantage of that to reiterate it. It is a reform that does not allow a political customization of the confirmed referendum on which citizens will be called upon to express their views, instead of requiring the political parties to discuss the referendum in its objectivity, substance and content. And it is a reform that conceives the Senate as a substantial Chamber of representation of territorial autonomy, although - in our opinion - this option could have had a more organic and consistent path with reference to the reality and role of the German Bundesrat. We do not consider it positive that, in essence, the Regions no longer have a concurrent legislative competence but, in fact, an implementing competence of less practical importance: the insertion of a new clause of supremacy of the State and, above all, the new limit of the new general norms of the State, which introduces in the ordinary Regions a kind of concurrent competence weakened, will not help to reduce the disputes. In any case, I believe that although the Senate functions are less incisive than the current ones, there are potentials to develop. Much depends on the choice of regions. If they send second or third row members to the Senate, it will become an irrelevant and, perhaps, useless organ. If, instead, it will be the seat where all the Governors of the 19 Regions and the two Autonomous Provinces will compare, then it will have a considerable political weight and assume the function of a real Chamber of Autonomouss. For us, representatives of the special autonomy, this reform protects the competences and powers in place, because it excludes the special regions from the application of the new Title V, requiring the revision of our statutes to a future constitutional law on the basis of agreement. In this way the pact clause was introduced for the first time in Republican history, which will allow regional assemblies to carry out a genuine reform initiative without having to fear unilateral impositions by Parliament. The international agreements, in particular the guarantees of the De Gasperi-Gruber agreement, are clearly still in force. It is, in essence, a reform that also guarantees the balanced representation of the Special Regions and the two Autonomous Provinces of Trento and Bolzano in the new Senate. The specificity of the Autonomous Province of Bolzano has also been taken into account, ensuring the representation of both the major linguistic groups, German and Italian. It is a reform that, with the reformulation of Article 116, introduced at the last reading here in the Senate, provides for the new and simplified procedure for the transfer of powers of itself state, such as the environment, applicable not only to ordinary regions but also to special virtuous regions. Finally, we must recognize the Government and the majority who have shown themselves sensitive to our essential requests, and I take this opportunity to thank Minister Boschi, Undersecretary Pizzetti and President Finocchiaro for their constant and great commitment. I therefore declare the Group's vote in favour. [[Applause from the groups (Aut (SVP, UV, PATT, UPT) - PSI-MAIE) and PD.]] (at 19.10 a.m.)

ParlaMint-IT_2016-01-20-LEG17-Senato-sed-563.ana.u137	Before giving the floor to Senator Torrisi, I would like to inform the House that, for those who need to hold meetings or to raise their voices a lot, there are comfortable sofas outside.
ParlaMint-IT_2016-01-20-LEG17-Senato-sed-563.ana.u138	I call Mr Torrisi. TORRISI (AP (NCD-UDC)). Madam President, Ministers and ladies and gentlemen, the People's Area Group will vote in favour of the reform of Part II of the Constitution, in order to overcome the bicameralism of equality and the revision of Title V, in the latter and decisive decision by the Senate. It is a reform that Italy has been waiting for for more than thirty years, after the failure of countless reformist attempts that have followed each legislature, starting with the first bicameral Commission Bozzi in 1983. We have already paid very high costs for the failure to modernise our institutional system, dating back to an outdated historical period, reforms that other major democracies have carried out for some time. Faced with the challenges of international competition and globalisation, a new failure is a luxury that Italy absolutely cannot afford. There is a very close connection between the institutional system and the economy, between a seven-year recession that has put social cohesion in crisis, on the one hand, and the weakness of political institutions, on the other. As a constitutionalist like Mario Dogliani pointed out two years ago, with reference to the fundamental rights provided for in the first part of our Charter: It is fair to say that the first and second parts of the Constitution cannot be separated. But the first can only be implemented if the second works. Meuccio Ruini, in 1947 in this House, spoke the following words: We are certain that the Constitution will last for a long time and may never end, but it will always be completed and adapted to the needs of historical experience. None of us can forget the real institutional crisis that emerged dramatically following the 2013 parliamentary elections, when the legislature seemed unable to start, precisely because of the bicameralism of equality and two Chambers elected even by two different electoral bodies. There was no majority in both chambers; one could not form a government or elect the new President of the Republic. We were in an institutional impasse from which we came out only thanks to the re-election of President Napolitano and the formation of a Convergence Government, with the fundamental objective of finally achieving, in this legislature, the reforms of the Constitution and the electoral system, as well as the fundamental objective of getting the country out of the economic crisis. Here I should like to express special thanks to the senators of the New Right Centre and the People's Area because, if we can achieve this great result today, it is due to those senators and those Members who, in October 2013, saved the legislature and reforms, with a painful and courageous act together, and remained and remain faithful to that solemn commitment made by all those who paid real ovations to Napolitano's speech, in front of Parliament in common session. The reform is not the result of improvisation, but of a thirty-year confrontation of cultural, academic and political positions. Its general facility corresponds to the conclusions expressed by a large majority by the Reform Commission, established by the Led Government. And if the examination started with a text from the Government, it must be emphasized that there were many important changes made to that text, the result of a significant parliamentary work, especially by the Senate, starting with the rapporteurs Finocchiaro and, in the first phase, Calderoli. The Senators of the People's Area contributed significantly to this work, on many decisive aspects, proposing - for example - first the solution for the election of the Senators, which was then, in essence, adopted. It is a reform to which, at least at first reading, the Italian Parliamentary Group of Forza Italia has contributed, while today it is opposed and some of its members resort even to alarmist hyperboles on the democratic emergency, which the reform would lead, forgetting the coincidence between the content of the reform and the electoral programmes and the positions expressed, also in the parliamentary, since 1994. The Senate is the creator of the reform, but also its main object. Let me give another thanks to all the senators, because today we are bringing to completion a historical fact, which gives credit to this House which, after thirty years, puts an end to a paradox considered insurmountable: that of the reformer who must reform himself. The Senate completely changes its nature, role and functions, but it is not a dequalified House at all. It is, indeed, a Chamber that will play a fundamental role in the new structure of our bicameralism: first, to represent the territorial authorities and to be the place of connection between the regional legislators and the state legislature, in particular through the competition to national legislation, in order to put an end to that abnormal constitutional dispute between the State and the Regions, which the hasty and ill-conceived amendment of 2001 has determined, with serious damage to the legal certainty and the economy of the country. The Senate will, however, carry out other important functions of liaison with the European Union, of evaluating public policies, of verifying the implementation of the laws of the State and of appointing two constitutional judges. Only the House of Deputies, as the seat of national representation, will grant and revoke confidence to the Government, as in other European democracies, thus putting an end to our equal bicameralism, which has so much harmed not only the stability and effectiveness of government action, but also the authority and centrality of the seat of popular sovereignty. The simplification and simplification of the legislative procedure, with certain times for the approval of the laws, are aimed at achieving a "governing democracy" that finally overcomes the inability of decision making that has long plagued the political-institutional system and that can endanger the functioning of democracy. It will be strengthened and made more effective the action of government, but also the confrontation and the parliamentary dialectic, since the reform overall maintains the form of parliamentary government. And also the system of guarantees is assured, thanks to the modification of several articles of the Charter, which provide for strong limits to the decree of urgency, the statute of oppositions, the preventive recourse of constitutional legitimacy of the electoral laws of Chamber and Senate, the quorum of the three fifths for the election of the President of the Republic after the third ballot, the guarantee of examination of the proposals of law of popular initiative, the lowering of the quorum of validity of the repealing referendum. Complementing the reform of bicameralism is the revision of Title V of the Constitution, which remedy - as already pointed out - the failures caused by the sudden amendment of 2001. Finally, the reduction in the costs of politics must not be overlooked, not only by reducing the number of senators, but also by limiting the expenditure of the Regional Councils, by abolishing the CNEL and the Provinces. We defend this reform not for partisan calculations, but in the interests of the country. That is why, when the majority that supported the reform was two-thirds larger, we first proposed that the reform should be subject to vote by voters through the referendum, for which many Members of the People's Area have already joined the yes committee promoted by Mr Adornato. On this point I address myself openly and directly to the President-in-Office of the Council and Mrs Boschi, as Mr Lupi has already done in the House. Our yes is to reform, to its content, to the benefits that it can bring to Italy and certainly not to a plebiscite about a person and his political action. With all the consideration and esteem of the person and his abilities, do not make the mistake of a personalization of the referendum, to which they push, not by chance, precisely those who fight for the no. The reform has been proposed and followed with great determination by the Government because - as I have already mentioned - it is the main objective, the same reason to be of the legislature. However, it is not just the legislature that is at stake; the fate of our institutions and their ability to respond to the needs of the country are at stake. The future of Italy is at stake. We are conducting a campaign for yes to the inclusive referendum, we are working on the broadest possible front, involving parliamentarians, representatives of civil society and simple citizens. A Constitution is not just a legal text, it is a legislative act. It is, first of all, a political and historical act. We change the Constitution if there is the historical need to do so and we believe, and believe, that that necessity exists, because we need to close the eternal institutional transition in our country as an instrument to relaunch Italy. We are beginning to have difficult years behind us because of a terrible economic crisis, the most serious since the post-war period; a crisis that has impoverished and weakened our country, which has created a social divide and increased inequalities among citizens. Therefore, as never before, politics today has to respond to these issues and it is by acting that a solution is found, because the real risk would be to do nothing. The President of the Republic, Sergio Mattarella, recently hoped that the reforms will come to an end in this legislature, since the sense of incompleteness would risk producing further uncertainties and conflicts, as well as nourishing mistrust. The reform that we approve today is the result of the work of a majority, of politically autonomous but complementary forces in this delicate and fundamental phase of our country's life, which have a single denominator: the reformative will. Madam President, ladies and gentlemen, I would like to start by recalling what Mr Vincenzo Cuoco wrote about the fact that there are no perfect constitutions: The Constitution is like a dress: if you're a hunchback, the dress that's good for you is a dress that more or less manages to handle or hide the hump that you have and it's not good for another but it's good for you only. The reform we are discussing is perhaps not the ideal one, but it is the best that can be done in this Parliament today. Therefore, and I conclude, Madam President, I believe that we are going to vote for a complex and completed reform text, which closes the troubled season of the second Republic and which intends to project a stronger country in the future, with stronger institutions, with stronger economic bases, with greater credibility in Europe and, therefore, with a greater chance of counting - as is right - in Europe and beyond. The People's Area Group, consistent with the responsibility it has assumed, contributing first to the birth of the Led Government and then to that of the Renzi Government, supports and shares this constitutional reform. [[Applause from the AP Group (NCD-UDC) and Senator Santini.]]

ParlaMint-IT_2016-01-20-LEG17-Senato-sed-563.ana.u144	ROMANI Paolo (FI-PdL XVII). Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, at last, in the substantial disinterest of the country, which warns of other more urgent priorities, the process of reform in the Senate comes to an end. We are talking about a reform on which only one in three Italians, according to a recent RAI survey, think they have clear ideas. It is a pity that this would have ended what could have been the great reform of the institutions, the historic opportunity to really change Italy, to make our democracy freer, more European, more efficient. It should have been an important debate and it has been reduced in this last step to a series of speeches in the middle of the night, due to the need to cage up the whole majority on the vote on the reform before the nominations in the Commissions. It was the great opportunity to demonstrate to the citizens, increasingly discouraged and disappointed, that politics is capable of self-generating, self-forming, of truly representing Italians. It could have been the great response to the widespread abstentionism, to the anti-politics of the protest. We had given our willingness for this great project. As my friend Quagliariello recalled yesterday, we really believed that it was possible to link legal and constitutional cultures that had been opposed for decades; that it was really time to unite the country to write together those new rules that Italy had and desperately needs. However, we had to take note that soon that great project turned out to be what it was: a small design of power functional only to the Democratic Party. A constitutional reform that eliminates the electivity of the Senate, an electoral law that will allow only one party to govern, even representing only 20% of the Italians; a transformation of the Provinces into second level institutions managed by the local bureaucracy and a reform of the RAI that supplies the public service firmly in the hands of the Government. We are at the paradox for which the consent of one in five Italians can suffice to have in hand all the power of our country. ParlaMint-IT_2016-01-20-LEG17-Senato-sed-563.ana.u145	I ask you to speak for an explanation of vote.

ParlaMint-IT_2016-01-20-LEG17-Senato-sed-563.ana.u147	FINOCCHIAR (PD). Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, I would like to begin by thanking my Group, which allows me this explanation of vote to conclude a commitment which has certainly been the hardest and most difficult one, but also the most exciting of a long (in the opinion of some colleagues, too long) parliamentary experience that will end with this parliamentary term. I would like to begin with the words spoken by Meuccio Ruini on 22 December 1947, at the last sitting of the constituent Assembly. On that occasion, Meuccio Ruini, on the one hand, expressed the certainty that the Constitution would last for a long time. "Perhaps it will never end," he said. On the other hand, he said that he would adapt to changing times and experience; on the other hand, giving an overall assessment of this work, he gave a far from triumphalistic judgment. While he emphasized the greatness (so I would like to define it) and the success of the first part of the Constitution, he recognized that the second had presented serious difficulties and that they had not solved all the institutional problems: for example, for the composition of the two Chambers and the electoral system, which was put back to ordinary law. In short, an explicit indication of what already appeared to have failed in the Constitution, already at the solemn moment of its approval. Nor could it be otherwise. And today President Casini reminded us of that. If we contextualize that debate, let us remember that we were on the eve of the first elections and remember not only the tension between the two great mass parties, Christian Democracy and the Communist Party, but the fact that they alluded to a truly alternative political, cultural and social system. And each of the two parties, of almost similar consistency, feared that in the successive elections, the first free elections of the Republic, one force could prevail over the other. It was thus that, after a debate that has not been mentioned in this House today, but not even during the debate these days, if not very few interventions, choosing between very different solutions and changing each political party and each exponent even their original position (the DC started from a regionalist approach while the PCI started from a monocameralist approach), it came to a solution that was that of a perfect bicameralism: two Chambers that had the same functions, both linked by a trusted relationship to the Government, but elected with different electoral systems and with different active and passive electorate. What was introduced into the order was an element of blockage, an element of farraginosity, an attempt to prevent the victory of a part from being a complete victory. This theme, already explained in the final report of 22 December 1947, is continuously taken up by the same constituents in the following years, and repeatedly. We have an extraordinary testimony in the reconstruction of that conversation between Elijah and Scoppola, on the one hand, and Dossetti and Lazzati on the other, which is from 1984, but which was published years later. As early as 1951, Dossetti returned to this question and, speaking of the constitutional system, said: It has been structurally arranged on the premise of a mutual counterweight and therefore of a complex, slow and rare operation, such as that of a State that had nothing to do but few and infrequent acts both regulatory and executive, because it does not have to carry out an action of mediation (...) and much less a continuous action of reformation, of propulsion of the social body. Basically, this theme is then at the centre, for example, of Mortati's reflections (of those delivered between 1972 and 1974), which, speaking of bicameralism, says: It is a problem to which it cannot be too late to give a solution. And if we trace the republican history of this issue, which has been absent from the debate in this House and which is the first reason why this reform is being done, we continue to discuss in the sixty-seven, sixty-eight, seventy years of the Republic, trying to remedy it with different instruments, the first already in the fifties is the electoral law with the majority prize. And if it is abandoned it is not only because of the reactions that led to the law fraud - many colleagues have remembered it - but also because the political strategy changes, and the strengthening of the government by Christian Democracy is instead pursued with a proportional system that can multiply the political subjects and alliances. But the problem is always the same and it is that of a defect in the Parliament-majority-Government circuit that makes governments weak, that makes governments unable to assume that function of propulsion of the overall system of the country that we now record with such tragic evidence, also because the context has changed and the strength and competitiveness of our national system needs a further push. Moreover, in the same attempt to remedy that original defect, we can refer to attempts to strengthen, for example, the President of the Republic, on which constitutionalists and politicians were busy, or the use of semi-presidency, which has been a constant in the Italian debate since the 1970s. Because the point - a colleague or colleague of the 5 Star chin recalled it yesterday - was, as Zagrebelsky identified, to define a new point of balance between inconclusion and force, I repeat between inconclusion and force. And that is exactly what we are trying to do, finally, after so many decades of doing: to find a new point of balance. But here we have spoken of something else, in a confusion - look, colleagues - which, in my opinion, represents a highly explosive mixture in a country that has the characteristics that Senator Tronti has spoken about today and that sees this relationship between citizens and politics so difficult, harsh, sometimes polluted. I understand that the 5 Star Movement has done and makes the assembly its identity figure, but I think that perhaps because of its youth this movement also needs to purify a little and better define its own political culture. Because I have heard incredible things in this House: on the one hand this exaltation of representativeness and on the other hand the mortification of some representations. I am thinking of what has been said about the directly elected regional councillors and, in general, about the caste of the elected, with judgments that are both summing up and very often unjust. On the one hand, the legitimacy of this Government and this President-in-Office of the Council, who has never been elected (as if the current Constitution required this, but does not require it at all) has been contested; on the other hand, however, there has been a damnation against the forms of premiered. On the one hand, it has been argued that costs have been abolished throughout the entire phase of the electoral campaign that led to the electoral affirmation of the 5 Star Movement, on the other hand today it is claimed that this cost abolition has mortified representation. I frankly believe that we should think with greater coldness and return to the questions that few interventions today - I am referring to the interventions of Senator Fornaro, Senator Chiti and Senator Casini - have tried to focus. But what actually works, it seemed to me, in this debate is exactly the prevention that inspired the constituents, that worked during the drafting of the Constitution and that ultimately prevailed; I am referring to the temptation to perpetuate the weakness of our system. Frankly - as I have said many times in this House - I have never been convinced, during this long parliamentary debate, not even the idea that a Senate elected directly, without relations with regional institutions, could be a counterweight, a Senate of guarantees. This is certainly an attractive prospect, but it is absolutely eager to think that 95 people directly elected (and by proportional method), but not narrowed by a trust bond, can be a counterweight. Compared to what? Compared to a House in which the bond of majority and the weight of the majority can allow to face the many issues and the many challenges of a ruling democracy - I want to use this very term - that our country needs. Inexplicable is the position of the colleagues of Forza Italia. I appreciated the elusive elegance with which Senator Romani made his explanation of vote today and I must say that I was fascinated by the rutilant well-alternism of the intervention of Senator Gasparri, who spoke about everything except constitutional reform. [[Applause from the PD Group.]] That is the best text possible, ladies and gentlemen. Probably each of us and myself - as President Renzi said today - have exercised some critical activity on the original approach of the text of the government bill. It is the best fruit of the best possible transaction and therefore is the best law possible. I do not think it is necessary here to explain its content to you (we have done so many times in this House), nor do I think it is worth stressing the historical importance of this moment for me. But I want to say one thing, even to those who have not voted for this text and will not vote for it and to those who voted for it and will not vote for it tonight. I appreciated a reference made yesterday by Mr Mussini, who said that institutional forms cannot be merely forms. The Greeks said that democracy is a practice. Well, I think that's the way it is. If we approve this text, if the House will approve it again, if the referendum will confirm it, it will be up to each of us, majority and opposition, political ruling class of this country, to make it live and make it live to the best of the potential that this Senate will have, in its power of representation of the territorial institutions and their weight... Stop! [[Comments from the PD Group.]]
